A. Knowledge Based Questions

I. Fill in the blanks.

  1. Colonialism is a system in which one country controls and exploits another region by imposing its own political, economic, and cultural systems.

  2. The Age of Exploration began in the 15th century when European powers like Spain, Portugal, and Britain started expanding across Asia, Africa, and the Americas.

  3. The main causes of colonial expansion were political rivalry, economic greed, religious motives, and the desire for scientific inquiry and exploration.

  4. European colonialism caused serious effects such as loss of independence, resource exploitation, and the imposition of foreign values on colonised societies.

  5. Vasco da Gama reached Calicut in 1498, opening the sea route to India and marking the beginning of Portuguese presence on Indian soil.

  6. The Battle of Plassey in 1757 marked the turning point when the British East India Company gained control over Bengal.

  7. The Doctrine of Lapse, introduced by Lord Dalhousie, allowed the British to annex any Indian state whose ruler died without a natural male heir.

  8. The Revolt of 1857, led by soldiers, peasants, and rulers, is remembered as India's first large-scale struggle for independence.

II. State true or false for the following statements.

  1. Colonialism was mainly about cultural exchange and peaceful cooperation among nations. — False (Colonialism was about one country controlling and exploiting another for its own benefit, often through violence, conquest, and exploitation.)

  2. The Portuguese, Dutch, French, and British arrived in India at the same time and with equal control. — False (They arrived at different times — the Portuguese in 1498, the Dutch in the early 17th century, the British in the early 17th century, and the French in 1668. They did not have equal control; the British eventually dominated.)

  3. The Dutch were mainly interested in trading and avoided territorial conquest. — True (The Dutch were mainly focused on commercial dominance, especially in the spice trade, and did not focus on conquering territories.)

  4. The British East India Company received its first charter from Queen Elizabeth I. — True

  5. The Doctrine of Lapse didn't respect the Indian tradition of adoption for royal succession. — True

  6. The policy of Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers militarily stronger and independent. — False (The Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers militarily weaker and completely dependent on the British. They had to give up their own armies and pay for British troops stationed in their states.)

  7. The Bengal Famine of 1770 was made worse by the British policy of high taxation and cash crops. — True

  8. The Revolt of 1857 was limited only to Indian soldiers and did not involve civilians. — False (The revolt involved peasants, artisans, dispossessed landlords, rulers, and civilians — not just soldiers. It was a large-scale movement against colonial rule.)

III. Give one word for the following.

  1. The practice of one country exploiting another's resources and ruling over it. — Colonialism

  2. The Indian queen who bravely fought the British after the annexation of her state under the Doctrine of Lapse. — Rani Lakshmibai (of Jhansi)

  3. The British Governor-General who introduced the Doctrine of Lapse. — Lord Dalhousie

  4. The economic theory explaining how India's wealth was systematically taken to Britain. — Drain of Wealth

  5. The 19th-century British official whose education policy aimed to create English-speaking Indians to serve the colonial government. — Thomas Babington Macaulay

B. Understanding Based Questions

I. Define the following.

1. Colonialism

Colonialism is the practice of one country taking control over another country's land and people. The stronger country rules over the weaker one, exploits its resources for profit, and imposes its own political, economic, and cultural systems on the colonised people. The colonisers not only ruled the land but also tried to change the way people lived, worked, and thought.

2. Doctrine of Lapse

The Doctrine of Lapse was a policy introduced by Lord Dalhousie in the 19th century. According to this policy, if an Indian ruler died without a natural male heir, his state would be automatically annexed by the British East India Company. The British refused to recognise the Indian tradition of adopting an heir. Using this policy, the British annexed several states including Jhansi, Satara, and Nagpur.

3. Decolonisation

Decolonisation is the process through which countries that were under colonial rule gained independence and self-rule. After World War II weakened European colonial powers, powerful nationalist movements grew across Asia, Africa, and other regions. As a result, many colonies gained independence — for example, India became independent in 1947, followed by many African countries in the 1950s and 1960s.

II. Differentiate between the following.

1. Direct and Indirect rule as practised by European powers.

Direct Rule Indirect Rule
The coloniser directly governs the territory with its own officials and administration. The coloniser controls the territory through local rulers who act as puppets under colonial influence.
The colonised people have no local representation in governance. Local rulers appear to be in charge but are controlled by a British Resident or colonial advisor.
Example: After the Battle of Buxar, the British directly collected revenue (Diwani rights) and administered Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa. Example: Under the Subsidiary Alliance, Indian rulers like the Nizam of Hyderabad appeared independent but were actually controlled by the British Resident placed in their courts.
Complete loss of sovereignty for the colonised region. Sovereignty is reduced to a mere show — rulers bear costs while the coloniser enjoys power.

2. Portuguese and British methods of colonisation in India.

Portuguese Methods British Methods
Arrived first in 1498 (Vasco da Gama at Calicut). Arrived in the early 17th century as traders.
Focused on capturing strategic ports (e.g., Goa in 1510) and building naval bases. Focused on setting up trading factories (Surat, Madras, Bombay, Calcutta) and gradually expanding territory.
Used the cartaz system — compulsory passes for ships — to monopolise the spice trade. Used policies like Doctrine of Lapse, Subsidiary Alliance, and 'divide and rule' to expand control.
Actively spread Christianity through the Goa Inquisition (1560), involving forced conversions and temple destruction. Focused more on economic exploitation and political control rather than religious conversion.
Did not expand deep into the Indian subcontinent; remained mainly coastal. Eventually conquered almost the entire Indian subcontinent and ruled for nearly two centuries.
Declined by the 17th century with the arrival of stronger rivals. Dominated India until 1947.

III. Choose the correct option.

1. Which of the following was NOT a major European colonial power in India?
Answer: c. Italy

2. The Portuguese introduced which system to control trade in the Arabian Sea?
Answer: b. Cartaz

3. The Battle of Colachel (1741) marked the defeat of which European power by Travancore?
Answer: a. Dutch

4. The British used the policy of 'divide and rule' mainly to:
Answer: c. Prevent Indian unity and expand their control

5. The first major peasant movement against the British indigo planters occurred in:
Answer: a. Bihar

6. The Great Rebellion of 1857 resulted in:
Answer: a. End of Mughal rule and beginning of British Crown rule

C. Application/Concept Based Questions

I. Answer the following questions in brief.

1. What were the main causes of European colonial expansion during the Age of Exploration?

The main causes of European colonial expansion were:
- Political Competition: European nations competed to prove their power and prestige by capturing colonies. The more colonies a nation controlled, the more respected and feared it became.
- Economic Advantages: Colonies offered abundant raw materials like spices, cotton, silk, gold, and silver. European countries gained huge profits by taking resources cheaply and selling manufactured goods back at high prices.
- Religious Motives: Christian missionaries and European rulers felt it was their duty to spread Christianity. They built churches and converted local people, often calling it a 'civilising mission.'
- Scientific Inquiry and Exploration: Explorers like Vasco da Gama, Columbus, and Magellan were motivated by the desire to discover new lands, sea routes, and knowledge about the world.

2. How did religious motives help Europeans justify colonial domination?

Religious motives helped Europeans justify colonial domination through the idea of a 'civilising mission.' European rulers and Christian missionaries claimed they were bringing progress and modernity to 'backward' societies. They described local populations as 'savage,' 'primitive,' or 'barbaric' to justify their dominance. By spreading Christianity, building churches, and converting local people, they weakened existing cultures and traditions. Once people gave up their traditional beliefs, they became easier to influence and rule. Thus, religion was used both as a justification for conquest and as a tool to strengthen political control.

3. Describe the importance of the Battle of Plassey (1757) in the establishment of British rule in India.

The Battle of Plassey (1757) was a major turning point in Indian history because:
- The British East India Company, led by Robert Clive, defeated the Nawab of Bengal, Siraj-ud-Daulah, with the secret support of Mir Jafar, who betrayed the Nawab.
- This victory gave the Company control over Bengal, the richest province in India. Bengal's huge revenue, fertile land, and trade resources became the foundation of British power.
- The Company emerged as a 'kingmaker,' deciding which Indian rulers would stay in power.
- It marked the beginning of British political dominance in India, transforming them from traders into rulers.
- The massive wealth gained from Bengal (called 'Plassey Plunder') helped finance Britain's Industrial Revolution.

4. What was the impact of the Doctrine of Lapse on Indian princes and people?

The Doctrine of Lapse, introduced by Lord Dalhousie, had a severe impact:
- Indian rulers who died without a natural male heir had their states annexed by the British, ignoring the centuries-old tradition of adoption.
- States like Jhansi, Satara, and Nagpur were annexed, reducing Indian kingdoms to conquered land.
- It deeply hurt the pride of rulers and their people, as it ignored Indian customs and traditions.
- It created widespread anger and distrust towards the British.
- This resentment played an important role in sparking the Revolt of 1857, when Indians rose up against British rule.

5. Explain two ways in which British economic policies led to famines in India.

Two ways in which British economic policies led to famines:
1. High taxation and cash crop farming: Company officials imposed very high cash taxes on farmers, demanding payment regardless of whether harvests were good or bad. Farmers were forced to grow cash crops like indigo and cotton instead of food grains, leaving little to eat during shortages. When poor harvests struck in 1770, the Bengal Famine followed, killing nearly 10 million people. The Company did not reduce taxes and even raised them during the famine.
2. Grain exports during famine: During the Great Famine of 1876–78, the colonial government followed a 'free market' policy, refusing to control food prices. About one million tonnes of rice were exported to Britain every year while people starved in India. Traders hoarded grain to increase prices, and relief was deliberately kept inadequate. The Viceroy, Lord Lytton, even organised a lavish Delhi Durbar while millions went hungry.

6. Who were Rani Lakshmibai and Begum Hazrat Mahal, and how did they contribute to the Revolt of 1857?

Rani Lakshmibai of Jhansi: When the British tried to annex her kingdom of Jhansi through the Doctrine of Lapse, she strongly opposed it and refused to surrender. She gathered soldiers, trained women to fight, and inspired common people with her leadership. With the support of Tatya Tope, she escaped from a surrounded Jhansi, captured the Gwalior fort, and fought bravely against the British. She was killed on 18 June 1858 while fighting on the battlefield. Even her enemies called her the 'best and bravest of the rebels.' She became a symbol of courage and patriotism.

Begum Hazrat Mahal of Awadh: After the British annexed Awadh and sent her husband, Nawab Wajid Ali Shah, away, she took charge and led the struggle in Lucknow. She organised soldiers, nobles, and common people to defend her kingdom. She led the defence of Lucknow against British forces and refused safe passage when offered. She warned Indians not to trust British promises. Even when forced to seek shelter in Nepal, she never gave up her spirit of resistance. She is remembered for her leadership, wisdom, courage, and sacrifice.

II. Answer the following questions in detail.

1. Discuss the political, economic, religious, and scientific factors that caused European colonial expansion.

Political Competition: European nations competed fiercely to prove their power and prestige. Capturing colonies became a way to display strength. Spain and Portugal divided South America between themselves through a treaty, while Britain and France competed for control over India and Africa. The more colonies a nation controlled, the more respected and feared it became. This race for dominance led to wars, naval battles, and long rivalries.

Economic Advantages: Colonies offered abundant raw materials not available in Europe — spices, cotton, silk, sugar, coffee, gold, and silver. European countries first set up trading posts but soon took complete control to secure steady supplies. Colonies were also forced to buy manufactured goods from European factories, giving the colonisers huge profits both ways. The wealth from colonies helped fund the Industrial Revolution in Europe, making these countries even stronger.

Religious Motives: Many European rulers and Christian missionaries felt it was their duty to spread Christianity in newly conquered lands. They built churches, converted local people, and tried to change traditional cultural practices. This was presented as a 'civilising mission,' bringing progress to 'backward' societies. In reality, spreading religion was a way to establish greater control — once people gave up their traditional beliefs, they became easier to rule.

Scientific Inquiry and Exploration: The Age of Colonialism was closely linked with exploration and scientific curiosity. Explorers like Vasco da Gama (sea route to India), Christopher Columbus (Americas), and Ferdinand Magellan (first circumnavigation of the world) played a huge role in expanding geographical knowledge. Europeans wanted to study the geography, climate, plants, animals, and minerals of new regions. These discoveries connected continents for the first time but also gave Europeans the advantage of controlling global trade and politics.

2. Describe the arrival and establishment of the Portuguese, Dutch, and French trading powers in India and analyse how they competed for supremacy before the rise of the British.

Portuguese in India: The Portuguese were the first Europeans to arrive in India by sea. In May 1498, Vasco da Gama reached Kappad (near Kozhikode, Kerala) after sailing around Africa's Cape of Good Hope. They captured Goa in 1510, making it their colonial capital. They introduced the cartaz system — compulsory passes for ships in the Arabian Sea — to monopolise the spice trade for nearly a century. They also tried to spread Christianity through the Goa Inquisition (1560). However, Indian rulers like Rani Abbakka Chowta of Ullal bravely resisted them. By the 17th century, their monopoly was broken by stronger rivals.

Dutch in India: The Dutch arrived in the early 17th century, mainly interested in commercial dominance in the spice trade. In 1602, they set up the Dutch East India Company (VOC), one of the wealthiest trading companies in the world. They established trading posts at Surat, Bharuch, Cochin, Pulicat, Nagapattinam, and Masulipatnam. Their strongest base was in Malabar, Kerala, where they displaced the Portuguese. However, their biggest defeat came at the Battle of Colachel (1741), where King Marthanda Varma of Travancore decisively defeated them. After this, the Dutch shifted focus to Indonesia and their influence in India declined.

French in India: The French entered India later, setting up their first trading post at Surat in 1668 and their main base at Pondicherry in 1764. Under Governor-General Dupleix (1742–1754), they pioneered strategies like training Indian sepoys in European military techniques and interfering in local succession disputes. This led to the Carnatic Wars (1746–1763) with the British. The French achieved some early victories, including the capture of Madras in 1746, but the British eventually gained the upper hand due to their stronger navy and larger resources. The French were left with only Pondicherry and a few smaller enclaves.

Competition for Supremacy: All four European powers — Portuguese, Dutch, French, and British — competed fiercely for control over India's rich trade. The Portuguese dominated first but were displaced by the Dutch in the spice trade. The Dutch were then defeated at Colachel. The French and British fought the Carnatic Wars, which the British won. Eventually, the British emerged as the supreme power, using their superior navy, better organisation, and larger resources to dominate India for nearly two centuries.

3. Explain the economic impact of British rule on India, including famines, the drain of wealth, and industrial decline.

Famines: India suffered some of the worst famines in history under British rule. After gaining Diwani rights of Bengal in 1757, the East India Company imposed very high cash taxes on farmers. Farmers were forced to grow cash crops like indigo and cotton instead of food grains. The Bengal Famine of 1770 killed nearly 10 million people — almost one-third of Bengal's population. The Company did not reduce taxes and even raised them during the famine. The Great Famine of 1876–78 killed up to 8 million people. During this famine, about one million tonnes of rice were exported to Britain every year while people starved. The Viceroy, Lord Lytton, organised a lavish Delhi Durbar while millions went hungry. Over the entire colonial period, historians estimate that between 50 and 100 million people died in these famines.

Drain of Wealth: The 'Drain of Wealth' theory, explained by leaders like Dadabhai Naoroji and Romesh Chunder Dutt, described how India's money, resources, and profits were regularly taken to Britain without any benefit to Indians. Naoroji, in his book Poverty and Un-British Rule in India (1901), used official British data to prove this. Economist Utsa Patnaik estimated that between 1765 and 1938, India lost nearly 45 trillion U.S. dollars (in today's value) to Britain — about 13 times the size of Britain's GDP in 2023. India was made to pay for colonial expenses like railways, telegraphs, and British wars. This continuous outflow left India poor and underdeveloped while Britain grew rich and industrialised.

Industrial Decline: Before British rule, India was one of the most advanced manufacturing regions in the world, famous for textiles, iron, steel, paper, and handicrafts. British policies destroyed these industries: heavy taxes were imposed on Indian textiles entering Britain, while British cloth was imported into India with little or no tax. This made Indian goods expensive and British goods cheap. By the 19th century, India's textile exports collapsed. Governor-General William Bentinck remarked in 1834: 'The bones of the cotton weavers are bleaching the plains of India.' By 1800, Indian textiles made up nearly 25% of world cloth trade, but by 1850, this dropped to less than 2%. Skilled workers lost their jobs and were forced into farming on heavily taxed land. By 1947, India's share of world GDP had fallen to barely 5%.

4. Describe the changes introduced by the British in education and governance, and their effects on Indian society.

Changes in Education:
Before British rule, India had diverse education systems — pathshalas, madrasas, viharas, gurukuls, and apprenticeship systems. British surveys found 100,000 to 150,000 schools running in Bengal and Bihar alone up to 1830. A major turning point came in 1835 when Thomas Babington Macaulay introduced his Minute on Education. He declared European knowledge superior to Indian learning and aimed to create a class of Indians who would be 'Indian in blood and colour, but English in taste, in opinions, in morals and in intellect.' English replaced Indian languages as the medium of higher education. Traditional schools lost importance.

Effects on society:
- English education created a class of clerks, teachers, lawyers, and minor officials who served the colonial government, saving the British money.
- It weakened traditional systems of knowledge and disconnected Indians from their cultural roots.
- English education became associated with power, prestige, and job opportunities, while regional languages and local knowledge were undervalued.
- It created a growing divide: English-educated elites on one side and the masses with little access to education on the other.
- However, by 1857, universities at Calcutta, Bombay, and Madras became breeding grounds for the first generation of Indian lawyers, reformers, and freedom fighters.

Changes in Governance:
The British introduced a centralised administrative system that replaced traditional governance. They imposed a foreign legal system that replaced community-based justice through panchayats and community elders. The new courts were expensive, slow, and conducted in English or Persian, which most villagers could not understand.

Effects on society:
- Governance became less personal and less responsive.
- Only the wealthy or those with access to lawyers could afford justice; ordinary Indians remained helpless.
- People grew alienated from the legal system.
- The centuries-old tradition of community-based self-rule and justice disappeared.
- Indians had little voice in how they were governed, while the British enjoyed maximum authority.

5. Evaluate the causes, course, and consequences of the Great Rebellion of 1857, highlighting the role of its leaders.

Causes:
- Economic exploitation: Heavy taxes, forced cash crop farming, destruction of industries, and famines caused widespread suffering among peasants and artisans.
- Political causes: Policies like the Doctrine of Lapse (annexation of Jhansi, Satara, Nagpur) and Subsidiary Alliance angered Indian rulers. The annexation of Awadh and sending Nawab Wajid Ali Shah away created resentment.
- Military causes: Sepoys were unhappy with British interference in their religious customs. The Vellore Mutiny of 1806 was an early warning. The final spark came when rumours spread that new rifle cartridges were greased with cow and pig fat, insulting both Hindu and Muslim beliefs.
- Social and cultural causes: British interference in religious practices, disrespect for Indian traditions, and the imposition of foreign values created anger.

Course:
- At Barrackpore, Mangal Pandey attacked his British officers in protest and was executed.
- At Meerut, sepoys killed their officers and marched to Delhi, declaring the elderly Mughal emperor Bahadur Shah Zafar as their symbolic leader.
- The revolt spread rapidly to Kanpur, Lucknow, Jhansi, and Gwalior.
- Rani Lakshmibai of Jhansi fought bravely, gathered soldiers, trained women to fight, escaped from a surrounded Jhansi, captured Gwalior fort, and was killed fighting on 18 June 1858.
- Begum Hazrat Mahal led the defence of Lucknow, organised soldiers and common people, refused safe passage, and never gave up resistance even when forced to seek shelter in Nepal.
- Nana Saheb led the revolt in Kanpur, and Tantia Tope supported Rani Lakshmibai.
- The British, with better weapons, organisation, and reinforcements, launched a brutal counterattack. Delhi was recaptured in September 1857. The British burned villages, destroyed crops, and executed thousands.
- The rebels lacked unified leadership, proper planning, and modern equipment, which weakened their struggle.

Consequences:
- The revolt was eventually defeated, but it became a turning point in Indian history.
- It was the first large-scale movement showing Indians could rise together across caste, religion, and region.
- It planted the seed of Indian nationalism and inspired later leaders.
- In 1858, the British Crown took direct control of India from the East India Company, beginning the British Raj.
- The British reorganised the army to divide Indian soldiers by caste and region to prevent future unity.
- They shifted from aggressive expansion to strengthening control over existing territories.
- The revolt left behind a powerful legacy of unity, sacrifice, and resistance — remembered as the first major step toward India's independence.

6. Discuss the legacy of European colonialism in India, focusing on economic exploitation, cultural theft, and the rise of nationalism.

Economic Exploitation:
- India, once contributing nearly one-fourth of world GDP, was reduced to one of the poorest nations by 1947, with barely 5% of world GDP.
- The Drain of Wealth theory showed how India lost nearly 45 trillion U.S. dollars (in today's value) to Britain between 1765 and 1938.
- Indian industries like textiles, iron, and steel were destroyed by unfair trade policies. Indian textiles dropped from 25% of world cloth trade (1800) to less than 2% (1850).
- Farmers were forced to grow cash crops, leading to famines that killed between 50 and 100 million people over the colonial period.
- India was made to pay for its own exploitation — railways, telegraphs, and the colonial administration were all financed through Indian tax revenues.

Cultural Theft:
- The British imposed foreign values, systems, and culture on Indians. Local customs, languages, and traditions were dismissed as 'backward.'
- Macaulay's education policy replaced Indian languages with English and weakened traditional knowledge systems like gurukuls and pathshalas.
- Many Indian treasures and artefacts were taken to Europe — including the Koh-i-Noor diamond, bronze idols, and other cultural heritage items.
- The Portuguese destroyed temples during the Goa Inquisition. The French destroyed the Vedapurishwaran temple at Pondicherry in 1748.
- This created a sense of cultural inferiority among Indians and weakened their confidence in their own identity.

Rise of Nationalism:
- Despite the suffering, colonial rule also ignited Indian nationalism.
- English education, though introduced to serve British interests, created a class of educated Indians who later became lawyers, reformers, and freedom fighters.
- The universities at Calcutta, Bombay, and Madras became breeding grounds for the national movement.
- The Revolt of 1857 planted the seed of nationalism, showing that Indians could unite across caste, religion, and region.
- Leaders like Dadabhai Naoroji and Romesh Chunder Dutt exposed the economic exploitation through their writings.
- Cultural pride and the desire for self-respect gave strength to nationalist movements.
- After decades of struggle, India finally achieved independence in 1947, showing that foreign rulers could never destroy India's identity.

D. HOTS (Problem Solving Skills)

1. Imagine the British had never used the strategy of 'divide and rule.' How might the unity among Indian rulers and people have changed the course of India's struggle against colonial power?

If the British had never used 'divide and rule,' the course of India's history would have been very different:

  • United resistance: Indian rulers would have been able to form strong alliances against the British. Instead of fighting the British one by one, they could have presented a united front, making it much harder for the British to conquer India.
  • Stronger armies: Without the Subsidiary Alliance, Indian rulers would have maintained their own independent armies. Combined military strength would have posed a serious challenge to the British East India Company.
  • No puppet rulers: Without British interference in succession disputes and policies like the Doctrine of Lapse, Indian kingdoms would have remained stable and independent.
  • Faster end to colonial rule: A united India could have resisted colonial domination much more effectively. The British relied heavily on exploiting divisions between Indian rulers, religious communities, and social groups. Without these divisions, the British would have found it nearly impossible to control such a vast and diverse country.
  • Preserved unity: The deep social and religious divisions that the British encouraged would not have existed, making India more cohesive and stronger as a nation.
  • Earlier independence: It is likely that India would have gained independence much earlier, or the British might never have been able to establish full colonial rule at all.

However, it is also true that some Indian rulers had their own rivalries and conflicts. Complete unity would have required visionary leadership and mutual trust among all Indian kingdoms.

2. The building of railways and telegraphs was often called a 'gift of modernisation.' Do you think these developments were truly meant for India's progress or mainly for strengthening British control? Give reasons.

These developments were mainly meant for strengthening British control, not for India's progress. The reasons are:

  1. Purpose of railways: The railway network was designed to transport raw materials like cotton, coal, jute, and indigo from the interior of India to major ports (Bombay, Calcutta, Madras) for export to Britain. The routes were not built to serve Indian needs but to link resource-rich areas to ports.

  2. Flood of British goods: Railways made it easier to flood Indian markets with cheap, machine-made British goods, which ruined local industries and handicrafts.

  3. Military purpose: Railways allowed the British to move troops, weapons, and supplies quickly to defeat rebellions like the Revolt of 1857. This speed of movement gave them a major military advantage.

  4. Purpose of telegraphs: The telegraph system gave the British the ability to communicate quickly across long distances, especially during wars, revolts, or famines, ensuring that no challenge to their authority could spread for long.

  5. Indians paid for their own exploitation: The construction and maintenance of railways and telegraphs were financed through Indian tax revenues. Railway companies were guaranteed profits by the government, but when they ran into losses, Indian taxpayers covered the costs.

  6. Racial segregation: Trains had separate carriages for Europeans and Indians, showing that even infrastructure was designed to maintain colonial superiority.

  7. Neglect of welfare: Instead of investing in schools, hospitals, or irrigation for Indian farmers, revenue was diverted into maintaining the system of exploitation.

While railways and telegraphs did create some benefits — like connecting distant regions and allowing faster travel — these were side effects, not the primary purpose. The main goal was always to serve British economic and political interests.

E. Analysis Based Questions

1. Analyse how the Subsidiary Alliance System forced Indian rulers to depend on the British. How did this weaken India's political independence?

The Subsidiary Alliance System, introduced by Lord Wellesley around 1798, forced Indian rulers to depend on the British in several ways:

  • Military dependence: Indian rulers had to agree to keep British troops stationed in their states 'for protection' and pay the heavy cost of maintaining them. They had to give up the right to have their own independent armies. This meant they could no longer defend themselves without British help.
  • Political dependence: A British Resident was placed in the courts of Indian rulers. Although the rulers appeared independent on paper, the Resident guided or controlled most of their decisions. This reduced their sovereignty to a mere show.
  • Loss of alliance-making power: Rulers could not make alliances with other powers. Once a state entered the Subsidiary Alliance, it could never leave without facing a powerful British military response.
  • Financial burden: Rulers had to bear the costs of maintaining British troops, which drained their treasuries. If they could not pay, they often had to cede territory to the British.
  • No independent foreign policy: Rulers lost the right to negotiate or interact with other Indian or foreign powers independently.

How it weakened India's political independence:
- It made Indian rulers completely dependent on the British, reducing them to puppets.
- It prevented Indian rulers from forming united alliances against the British.
- It created an 'empire on the cheap' — the British controlled vast areas without directly ruling them, while the rulers bore all the costs.
- It eliminated the possibility of collective resistance, as each ruler was individually bound to the British.
- The first to accept was the ruler of Hyderabad in 1798, followed by Awadh and Mysore. Over time, this became one of the main reasons why the British were able to dominate most of India without facing united resistance.

2. Refer to the given picture and answer the following questions:

a. Which revolt is shown in the picture? Who were the main groups involved in this revolt?

The picture shows a tribal uprising against colonial forces. Based on the chapter's context and the illustration depicting tribal people with bows, arrows, and spears fighting against what appear to be European soldiers with muskets, this represents one of the tribal resistance movements against British colonial rule. The main groups involved were tribal communities and the British colonial forces.

b. What were the main causes that led to this tribal uprising? What slogan or vow did they take during their revolt?

The main causes of tribal uprisings against British rule included:
- Loss of traditional forest rights and land due to British forest laws and land revenue policies.
- Exploitation by colonial officials, moneylenders, and landlords who took over tribal lands.
- Disruption of traditional tribal way of life and self-governance.
- Heavy taxation and forced labour imposed on tribal communities.

(Note: The chapter text does not provide specific details about the exact slogan or vow for this particular revolt. Students should refer to additional chapter content or supplementary materials for the specific slogan.)

c. What was the outcome of this revolt? Did the rebels succeed in their goal?

Most tribal revolts against British rule were eventually suppressed due to the British superiority in weapons, organisation, and military strength. However, these uprisings demonstrated the courage and determination of tribal communities to resist colonial exploitation and inspired future movements for independence.

F. Real-life Application Based Questions

1. If you were living in India during the 18th century, which European power (Portuguese, Dutch, French, or British) do you think would have affected your life the most, and why?

If I were living in India during the 18th century, the British would have affected my life the most. The reasons are:

  • Widest reach: By the 18th century, the British were expanding rapidly across India. After the Battle of Plassey (1757) and Buxar (1764), they controlled Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa — the richest provinces. Their influence was spreading to other regions through the Subsidiary Alliance and the Doctrine of Lapse.
  • Economic impact: British economic policies — high taxes, forced cash crop farming, and the destruction of local industries — would have directly affected my livelihood if I were a farmer or artisan.
  • Famines: British policies caused terrible famines, like the Bengal Famine of 1770, which killed millions. If I lived in Bengal, my family and community would have been devastated.
  • Education and culture: The British introduction of English education and the weakening of traditional schools would have changed how my children were educated and how my culture was treated.
  • Governance: The British replaced traditional systems of justice (panchayats) with foreign courts that were expensive and conducted in unfamiliar languages.
  • Long-lasting rule: Unlike the Portuguese (mainly in Goa), the Dutch (mainly in coastal trading posts), and the French (mainly in Pondicherry), the British controlled most of India and ruled for nearly two centuries, affecting every aspect of life.

While the Portuguese, Dutch, and French had significant local impact in their respective areas, the British had the widest and deepest impact on the lives of most Indians.

2. Imagine your town was forced to grow only one cash crop, like indigo or cotton, instead of food grains — just like farmers during British rule. What problems do you think your community would face, and how would it affect people's daily lives?

If my town was forced to grow only one cash crop like indigo or cotton instead of food grains, my community would face many serious problems:

  • Food shortages: Since we could not grow enough food grains, there would not be enough to eat. We would have to buy food from outside at high prices, which many families could not afford.
  • Famines: During droughts or poor harvests, the situation would become deadly. Without food reserves, people would starve, just as millions did during the Bengal Famine of 1770 and the Great Famine of 1876–78.
  • Debt and poverty: Farmers would have to take loans to buy food and pay high taxes. Many would fall into debt traps and lose their land to moneylenders.
  • Soil damage: Cash crops like indigo damaged the soil. The fermentation process for indigo poisoned the soil, causing a decade-long decline in rice yields across Bengal. This would make our land less productive over time.
  • Loss of income: The prices of cash crops were controlled by the British and traders. Farmers were paid very little for their crops, while the profits went to the colonisers and traders.
  • Unemployment: Artisans and weavers who depended on local raw materials would lose their jobs if all resources were diverted to export crops.
  • Health problems: Malnutrition from lack of food would weaken the community, making people more vulnerable to diseases.
  • Forced labour: Farmers who could not meet the demands of planters were often beaten or forced to work without fair pay.
  • Migration: Many people would be forced to leave their villages in search of work and food, breaking families and communities apart.

This is exactly what happened to Indian communities under British rule, and it shows how colonial policies turned a prosperous land into one of poverty and suffering.

G. Competency Based Questions

I. Assertion-Reason Type Questions

1. Assertion (A): The Portuguese were the first Europeans to arrive in India by sea.
Reason (R): Vasco da Gama reached Calicut in 1498, opening the sea route between Europe and Asia.

Answer: a. Both Assertion (A) and Reason (R) are true and Reason (R) is the correct explanation of Assertion (A).

Explanation: The Portuguese were indeed the first Europeans to arrive in India by sea. Vasco da Gama reached Calicut (Kozhikode) in 1498 after sailing around Africa's Cape of Good Hope, opening the sea route between Europe and Asia. This directly explains why the Portuguese were the first to arrive.

2. Assertion (A): The Dutch were defeated by the forces of Travancore under Marthanda Varma in the Battle of Colachel.
Reason (R): After this defeat, the Dutch expanded their rule to cover the whole of southern India.

Answer: d. Assertion (A) is false but Reason (R) is true.

Wait — let me re-examine: Actually, Assertion (A) is true — the Dutch were indeed defeated by Travancore forces under Marthanda Varma at the Battle of Colachel in 1741. Reason (R) is false — after this defeat, the Dutch did NOT expand their rule; instead, they shifted their focus to Southeast Asia (Indonesia) and their influence in India declined.

Corrected Answer: c. Assertion (A) is true but Reason (R) is false.

3. Assertion (A): The French and the British competed fiercely for political control in India during the 18th century.
Reason (R): Both countries wanted to establish military superiority and monopolise trade in India.

Answer: a. Both Assertion (A) and Reason (R) are true and Reason (R) is the correct explanation of Assertion (A).

Explanation: The French and British did compete fiercely, leading to the Carnatic Wars (1746–1763). Both wanted military superiority and trade monopoly in India. This competition directly explains the assertion.

4. Assertion (A): The Great Rebellion of 1857 was completely successful in ending British rule in India.
Reason (R): It united many soldiers and civilians against British exploitation and became a symbol of India's struggle for freedom.

Answer: d. Assertion (A) is false but Reason (R) is true.

Explanation: The Great Rebellion of 1857 was NOT completely successful — it was eventually defeated by the British. However, Reason (R) is true: it did unite many soldiers and civilians and became a symbol and inspiration for India's future freedom struggle.

II. Case Study Based Questions

1. Why was the Battle of Plassey an important turning point in Indian history?

The Battle of Plassey (1757) was an important turning point because:
- It gave the British East India Company control over Bengal, the richest province in India.
- Bengal's huge revenue, fertile land, and trade resources became the foundation of British power in India.
- The Company emerged as a 'kingmaker,' deciding which Indian rulers would stay in power.
- It marked the beginning of British political dominance in India, transforming them from traders into rulers.
- The massive wealth gained from Bengal (called 'Plassey Plunder') helped finance Britain's Industrial Revolution.

2. How did the East India Company benefit from its control over Bengal?

The East India Company benefited in several ways:
- It gained the Diwani rights (revenue collection rights) of Bengal, Bihar, and Orissa after the Battle of Buxar (1764), making them rulers in practice.
- It could collect huge amounts of revenue from Bengal's fertile and wealthy lands.
- It controlled Bengal's trade in valuable goods like cotton, silk, and other products.
- It used Bengal's wealth and resources as the base for expanding British control over the rest of India.
- The fortune gained from Bengal was so massive that it was called 'Plassey Plunder' in England and sparked an investment boom that helped finance the Industrial Revolution.

3. What role did Mir Jafar play in helping the British win the battle?

Mir Jafar was one of the Nawab of Bengal's (Siraj-ud-Daulah's) commanders. Robert Clive made a secret agreement with Mir Jafar, promising to make him the next Nawab in exchange for betraying Siraj-ud-Daulah. During the battle at Palashi (Plassey), Mir Jafar's forces — which made up most of the Nawab's army — did not fight. Their betrayal allowed the British to win easily despite being outnumbered. Even today, the name 'Mir Jafar' is used in India as a synonym for traitor, showing how strongly this betrayal is remembered.

III. Logical/Structured Thinking Based Questions

1. The British introduced the policy of high land revenue and encouraged the cultivation of cash crops such as indigo and cotton instead of food grains. Which of the following most accurately describes the effect of this policy on Indian society?

Answer: b. It led to food shortages and deadly famines, as farmers were forced to grow non-edible crops for export.

Explanation: The chapter clearly states that farmers were forced to grow cash crops like indigo and cotton instead of food grains, causing food shortages and terrible famines. The Bengal Famine of 1770 killed nearly 10 million people, and the Great Famine of 1876–78 killed up to 8 million people. High taxation and cash crop farming were direct causes of these disasters.

2. Arrange the following key events related to the rise of European powers in India in the correct chronological order:

  • The arrival of Vasco da Gama at Calicut (1498)
  • The Battle of Plassey, marking the start of British political control (1757)
  • The setting up of the French base at Pondicherry (1668/1764)
  • The establishment of the Dutch East India Company (1602)

Answer: a. Arrival of Vasco da Gama → Establishment of the Dutch East India Company → French base at Pondicherry → Battle of Plassey

Explanation:
1. Vasco da Gama arrived at Calicut in 1498.
2. The Dutch East India Company (VOC) was established in 1602.
3. The French set up their first trading post at Surat in 1668 and their main base at Pondicherry (the chapter mentions 1764 for the organised base, but the French entered India after the Dutch).
4. The Battle of Plassey took place in 1757.

The correct chronological order is: Vasco da Gama (1498) → Dutch East India Company (1602) → French base at Pondicherry → Battle of Plassey (1757).

KEY TERMS (Summary)

Term Definition
Colonialism The practice of one country taking control over another country's land and people, ruling, exploiting resources, and forcing its own culture and system on the weaker one.
Imperialism The policy of building a large empire by conquering or controlling other countries for power, wealth, and influence.
Monopoly Complete control over trade or production by one person or company (e.g., the Portuguese cartaz system for spice trade).
Navigation The art and science of planning and controlling the route of ships, used by Europeans to explore new sea routes.
Missionaries People who travelled to other countries to spread Christianity, building churches and schools but also changing local beliefs.
Exploitation The unfair use of people and resources for profit (e.g., colonisers taking India's raw materials while leaving Indians poor).
Decolonisation The process through which colonised countries gained independence and self-rule (e.g., India in 1947).
Drain of Wealth A term used by Dadabhai Naoroji to describe how Britain took away India's money, raw materials, and profits, making India poor and Britain rich.

Section 3: Short Answer Questions

Q1. What was the 'civilising mission' and how did Europeans use it to justify colonialism?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** The 'civilising mission' was the claim by European colonisers that they were bringing progress and modernity to 'backward' societies. They described local populations as 'savage,' 'primitive,' or 'barbaric' to justify their dominance. In reality, spreading religion and foreign values was a way to establish greater control—once people gave up their traditional beliefs, they became easier to influence and rule.

Q2. How did silver mined in the Americas contribute to global trade during the colonial period?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** Silver mined in the Americas became the backbone of global trade. Much of it was used to buy Asian goods like silk and spices. This silver eventually flowed into India and China, connecting the economies of three continents—Europe, the Americas, and Asia—for the first time in history.

Q3. Why was the Battle of Colachel (1741) significant in Indian history?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** At the Battle of Colachel (1741), the forces of Travancore under King Marthanda Varma decisively defeated the Dutch both on land and at sea. The Travancore kingdom became one of the earliest examples in Asia where an Indian power successfully repelled a European colonial force, forcing the Dutch to shift their focus to Southeast Asia.

Q4. What strategies did Dupleix pioneer that were later used by the British?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** Joseph François Dupleix pioneered the strategy of training Indian sepoys in European military techniques and interfering in local succession disputes to install puppet rulers dependent on French support. This gave the French indirect control over Indian politics and these strategies were later adopted by the British.

Q5. Explain the concept of 'Plassey Plunder' and its significance for Britain.

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** The fortune the British gained from Bengal after the Battle of Plassey was so massive that it was called 'Plassey Plunder' in England. The sudden wealth sparked an investment boom in Britain and helped finance the Industrial Revolution, showing how deeply India's economy shaped Britain's rise as a world power.

Q6. How did the British use the policy of 'divide and rule' to expand their control?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** The British exploited divisions within Indian society by identifying and encouraging tensions between regional kingdoms, ruling houses, and even religious communities. They supported one side in succession disputes or rivalries and emerged as power brokers, gaining land, money, or loyalty in return. This made it harder for Indians to unite against them and ensured British dominance.

Q7. What was the Famine Commission of 1878–80's argument about relief, and why was it criticized?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** The Famine Commission of 1878–80 argued that relief should be deliberately kept low, as giving more might make the poor 'dependent on government help.' This was criticized because while millions starved, the colonial government followed a 'free market' policy, refused to control food prices, and even exported about one million tonnes of rice every year to Britain during the famine.

Q8. How did American historian Brooks Adams and Will Durant view British exploitation of India?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** In 1895, American historian Brooks Adams argued that Britain's Industrial Revolution—which made it the world's leading power—was partly financed by India's wealth. Will Durant, another historian, called this process the 'stolen wealth of India,' highlighting the deep exploitation of Indian resources by Britain.

Q9. What did Macaulay mean when he said he wanted Indians who would be 'Indian in blood and colour, but English in taste'?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** Macaulay's goal was not to promote Indian traditions but to create a class of Indians who would serve as loyal supporters of British rule—people who looked Indian but thought, behaved, and held opinions like the English. This would ensure a steady supply of clerks and minor officials for the colonial government while weakening traditional Indian knowledge systems.

Q10. How did the British reorganise the army after the Revolt of 1857 and why?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** After the 1857 revolt, the British reorganised the army to divide Indian soldiers by caste and region, making it harder for them to unite in the future. They also shifted their policies from aggressive territorial expansion to strengthening control over areas they already ruled, ensuring another large-scale rebellion could not occur.

Section 4: Descriptive / Long Answer Questions

Q1. Describe the arrival of the Portuguese in India, their methods of establishing control, and their cultural legacy.

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** The Portuguese were the first Europeans to arrive in India by sea. In May 1498, Vasco da Gama reached Kappad near Kozhikode in Kerala after sailing around Africa's Cape of Good Hope. Though initially welcomed, his aggressive methods damaged relations—on his second voyage in 1502, he seized, tortured, and killed Indian merchants and bombarded Calicut. The Portuguese captured Goa in 1510, making it their colonial capital and naval base. They introduced the *cartaz* system—a compulsory pass for ships in the Arabian Sea—to monopolise the spice trade. They also spread Christianity and established the Goa Inquisition in 1560, which persecuted Hindus, Muslims, Jews, and even Christian converts suspected of practising old beliefs. Rani Abbakka Chowta of Ullal bravely resisted their naval attacks. Their cultural legacy survives today in Goa, Daman, and Diu through architecture (Basilica of Bom Jesus, Se Cathedral—UNESCO World Heritage Sites), churches, festivals like the Goan carnival, music, food, and the Konkani language.

Q2. Explain the Subsidiary Alliance system in detail. How did it benefit the British and harm Indian rulers?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** The Subsidiary Alliance was introduced by Lord Wellesley around 1798. Under this system, Indian rulers had to agree to keep British troops stationed in their states 'for protection' and pay the heavy cost of maintaining them. Rulers also had to give up the right to have their own independent armies or make alliances with other powers. A British Resident was placed in the courts of Indian rulers to guide or control most of their decisions. On paper, the rulers looked independent, but in reality, the Resident controlled their decisions, making them completely dependent on the British. Hyderabad was the first to accept in 1798, followed by Awadh and Mysore. Once a state entered the alliance, it could never leave without facing British military response. For the British, it was an 'empire on the cheap'—they controlled vast areas without directly ruling them, while the rulers bore the costs. This system made Indian rulers weak and powerless and was one of the main reasons the British dominated most of India without facing united resistance.

Q3. Discuss the impact of British rule on Indian industries, citing specific examples and data from the chapter.

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** Before British rule, India was one of the most advanced manufacturing regions, famous for textiles, iron, steel, paper, and handicrafts. Indian cotton textiles, from ultra-thin muslins of Bengal to embroidered cloth from Gujarat, were exported worldwide. Under British rule, these industries suffered a sharp decline. Heavy taxes were imposed on Indian textiles entering Britain, while British cloth was imported into India with little or no tax, creating an unfair trade system. Britain also controlled shipping routes and exchange rates. By 1800, Indian textiles made up nearly 25% of the world's cloth trade, but by 1850, this dropped to less than 2%. The city of Dhaka, once called the 'Manchester of the East,' turned into a ghost town. Governor-General William Bentinck remarked in 1834: 'The bones of the cotton weavers are bleaching the plains of India.' Traditional iron, steel, and paper industries also collapsed. Millions of skilled workers became unemployed and were forced into overburdened agriculture. By 1947, India's share of world GDP had fallen to barely 5%, from its leading position before the 18th century.

Q4. Describe the causes, key events, and consequences of the Great Rebellion of 1857.

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** **Causes:** The rebellion resulted from decades of exploitation, heavy taxes, disrespect for Indian traditions, and policies like the Doctrine of Lapse and Subsidiary Alliance. An early warning was the Vellore Mutiny of 1806, when sepoys revolted against uniform rules violating religious customs. The final spark came in 1857 when rumours spread that new rifle cartridges were greased with cow and pig fat, insulting both Hindu and Muslim beliefs. **Key events:** Mangal Pandey attacked British officers at Barrackpore and was executed. At Meerut, sepoys killed their officers and marched to Delhi, declaring the Mughal emperor Bahadur Shah Zafar as their symbolic leader. The revolt spread to Kanpur, Lucknow, Jhansi, and Gwalior, with leaders like Rani Lakshmibai, Nana Saheb, Tantia Tope, and Begum Hazrat Mahal playing key roles. The British recaptured Delhi in September 1857 after brutal house-to-house massacres. **Consequences:** Though defeated due to lack of unified leadership and modern equipment, the revolt was a turning point—it was the first large-scale movement showing Indians could unite across caste, religion, and region. It planted the seed of Indian nationalism. In 1858, the British Crown took direct control from the East India Company, beginning the British Raj. The army was reorganised to divide soldiers by caste and region, and policies shifted from aggressive expansion to consolidating control.

Q5. Compare the roles of Rani Lakshmibai and Begum Hazrat Mahal in the Revolt of 1857.

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** **Rani Lakshmibai of Jhansi:** When the British tried to annex Jhansi through the Doctrine of Lapse, she strongly opposed it and refused to surrender. She gathered soldiers, trained women to fight, and inspired common people. With the support of Tatia Tope, she escaped from a surrounded Jhansi and captured the Gwalior fort. On 18 June 1858, she was killed while fighting bravely on the battlefield. Even her enemies called her the 'best and bravest of the rebels.' **Begum Hazrat Mahal of Awadh:** After the British annexed Awadh and sent her husband Nawab Wajid Ali Shah into forced exile, she took charge of the struggle in Lucknow. She organised soldiers, nobles, and common people to defend her kingdom and led the defence of Lucknow with great courage. When the British offered safe passage, she boldly refused. When the Queen's proclamation of 1858 promised equality and religious freedom, she warned Indians not to trust British promises. Even when forced to seek shelter in Nepal, she never gave up her spirit of resistance. Both women became powerful symbols of courage, sacrifice, and patriotism, inspiring future generations of freedom fighters.

Q6. Analyse how the introduction of railways and telegraphs in India served British colonial interests rather than Indian welfare.

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** The British introduced railways and telegraphs in the 19th century, presenting them as signs of progress. However, their real purpose was strengthening British control. The railway network was designed to transport raw materials (cotton, coal, jute, indigo, food grains) from India's interior to ports like Bombay, Calcutta, and Madras for shipment to Britain. Simultaneously, railways flooded Indian markets with cheap British machine-made goods, ruining local industries. The telegraph system allowed British rulers to communicate quickly across long distances during wars, revolts, or famines, ensuring no challenge to their authority could spread. Railways also served a major military purpose—troops, weapons, and supplies could be moved quickly to defeat rebellions like the Revolt of 1857. Traditional trade centres and routes were ignored; lines were planned to link resource-rich areas to ports. Crucially, the construction costs were taken from Indian tax revenues—Indians paid for projects that served the British. Railway companies were guaranteed profits, but when they ran into losses, Indian taxpayers covered the costs. The first railway line (1853, Bombay to Thane) even had separate carriages for Europeans and Indians, showing racial segregation. In short, Indians were made to pay for their own subjugation.

Section 5: Assertion-Reason Questions

Q1. Assertion (A): The wealth drained from India helped finance the Industrial Revolution in Britain.
Reason (R): Indian cotton, raw materials, and tax revenues were sent to Britain to build factories, industries, and railways.
A) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A.   B) Both A and R are true but R is not the correct explanation of A.   C) A is true but R is false.   D) A is false but R is true.

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** A) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A.

Q2. Assertion (A): The British introduced English education in India to promote traditional Indian knowledge systems.
Reason (R): Macaulay believed that European knowledge was far superior to Indian learning.
A) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A.   B) Both A and R are true but R is not the correct explanation of A.   C) A is true but R is false.   D) A is false but R is true.

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** D) A is false but R is true. *(The British introduced English education to create loyal clerks for the colonial government, not to promote Indian knowledge. Macaulay did believe European knowledge was superior.)*

Q3. Assertion (A): The Battle of Plassey gave the British control over Bengal, the richest province in India.
Reason (R): Mir Jafar's betrayal of Siraj-ud-Daulah allowed the British to win despite being outnumbered.
A) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A.   B) Both A and R are true but R is not the correct explanation of A.   C) A is true but R is false.   D) A is false but R is true.

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** A) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A.

Section 6: Case Study Based Questions

Q1. Read the following passage and answer the questions:

"During the medieval period, India contributed nearly one-fourth of the world's GDP. This enormous wealth came from a strong agricultural system, thriving trade, and skilled artisans. The textiles of Bengal and Gujarat, such as fine muslin and colourful cotton fabrics, were in great demand in European markets. Spices from the Malabar and Coromandel coasts—including pepper, cardamom, and cloves—were valued almost like gold in Europe."

a. What was India's contribution to the world GDP during the medieval period?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** India contributed nearly one-fourth (25%) of the world's GDP during the medieval period.

b. Why were Indian textiles considered luxury items in Europe?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** Indian textiles, such as fine muslin from Bengal and colourful cotton fabrics from Gujarat, were so soft, light, and beautifully designed that they were considered luxury items in European markets.

c. Why were spices from the Malabar and Coromandel coasts valued 'almost like gold' in Europe?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** Spices like pepper, cardamom, and cloves were valued almost like gold in Europe because they were used to preserve food, add rich flavours, and even in medicines.

Q2. Study the following information and answer:

"The British introduced the Doctrine of Lapse under Lord Dalhousie. According to Indian traditions, if a ruler had no natural male heir, he could adopt a son who would inherit the throne. Under the Doctrine of Lapse, the British refused to recognise adopted heirs. Using this policy, the British annexed several important states, including Jhansi, Satara, and Nagpur."

a. Who introduced the Doctrine of Lapse and what was its main provision?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** Lord Dalhousie introduced the Doctrine of Lapse. Its main provision was that if a ruler died without a natural male heir, his state would be automatically annexed by the East India Company, as adopted heirs were not recognised.

b. Which Indian states were annexed using this policy?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** Jhansi, Satara, and Nagpur were annexed using the Doctrine of Lapse.

c. How did this policy contribute to the Revolt of 1857?

Show Answer
✅ **Answer:** The Doctrine of Lapse created widespread anger and distrust towards the British. Many people saw it as an unfair policy and an insult to Indian culture and traditions. This resentment played an important role in sparking the 1857 Revolt, when Indians rose up against British rule.

Q1. Who was the first European explorer to reach India by sea in 1498?

A. Zamindari B. Cartaz C. Mansabdari D. Ryotwari
Show Answer

Q3. Which battle marked the turning point when the British East India Company gained control over Bengal?

A. Battle of Buxar (1764) B. Battle of Plassey (1757) C. Battle of Colachel (1741) D. Battle of Panipat (1761)

Q4. Who introduced the Doctrine of Lapse in India?

A. Lord Wellesley B. Lord Dalhousie C. Lord Bentinck D. Lord Curzon
Show Answer

✅ Lord Dalhousie

Q5. The Battle of Colachel (1741) marked the defeat of which European power by the forces of Travancore under King Marthanda Varma?

A. French B. British C. Dutch D. Portuguese
Show Answer

✅ Dutch

Q6. Who was the Governor-General of French India from 1742 to 1754 who pioneered strategies like training Indian sepoys?

A. Joseph Francois Dupleix B. Robert Clive C. Lord Wellesley D. Lord Dalhousie
Show Answer

✅ Joseph Francois Dupleix

Q7. Who explained the concept of 'Drain of Wealth' from India to Britain in his famous book 'Poverty and Un-British Rule in India'?

A. Romesh Chunder Dutt B. Dadabhai Naoroji C. Brooks Adams D. Will Durant
Show Answer

✅ Dadabhai Naoroji

Q8. Which British official introduced the Minute on Education in 1835 promoting English education in India?

A. Lord Dalhousie B. Thomas Babington Macaulay C. Lord Wellesley D. William Bentinck
Show Answer

✅ Thomas Babington Macaulay

Q9. What was the Subsidiary Alliance introduced by Lord Wellesley?

A. A trade agreement between Indian rulers and the British B. A system where Indian rulers had to keep British troops in their states for protection and pay for their maintenance C. A policy to annex states without a male heir D. A military alliance against the French
Show Answer

✅ A system where Indian rulers had to keep British troops in their states for protection and pay for their maintenance

Q10. What was the immediate spark that triggered the Revolt of 1857 among the sepoys?

A. The imposition of the Subsidiary Alliance B. Rumours that new rifle cartridges were greased with cow and pig fat C. The annexation of Awadh D. The introduction of English education
Show Answer

✅ Rumours that new rifle cartridges were greased with cow and pig fat

Q11. Who was Rani Abbakka Chowta and what is she known for?

Show Answer

✅ Rani Abbakka Chowta was the queen of Ullal (near Mangaluru, Karnataka) who bravely fought against repeated Portuguese naval attacks in the 16th century, becoming a legendary figure and symbol of courage and defiance against foreign power.

Q12. Name two brave heroines of the Revolt of 1857 and briefly describe their contributions.

Show Answer

✅ Rani Lakshmibai of Jhansi opposed the annexation of her kingdom through the Doctrine of Lapse, gathered soldiers, trained women to fight, and died bravely in battle on 18 June 1858. Begum Hazrat Mahal of Awadh led the defence of Lucknow against British forces after her husband was sent away, organised soldiers and common people, and refused to surrender even when offered safe passage.

Q13. What was the 'cartaz' system introduced by the Portuguese?

Show Answer

✅ The cartaz was a compulsory pass or permit that Indian and foreign ships had to obtain to sail in the Arabian Sea. Ships without permits were attacked or seized and their goods were confiscated, allowing the Portuguese to monopolise the spice trade between India and Europe.

Q14. How did the British railways and telegraph systems primarily serve colonial interests rather than Indian welfare?

Show Answer

✅ The railways were mainly designed to transport raw materials from the interior to ports for export to Britain and to flood Indian markets with cheap British goods. The telegraph allowed quick communication during wars, revolts, or famines to suppress challenges to authority. Both were financed by Indian tax revenues but mainly benefited British trade, administration, and military control.

Q15. What was the Goa Inquisition and when was it abolished?

Show Answer

✅ The Goa Inquisition was established in 1560 by the Portuguese to persecute Hindus, Muslims, Jews, and even Christian converts suspected of practising old beliefs. Many temples were destroyed, forced conversions took place, and harsh punishments created fear. It was finally abolished in 1812.

Q17. Machines and money are examples of capital, which are man-made resources used in production. [Chapter_07]
Q18. Entrepreneurship does not involve taking risks or introducing new ideas. [Chapter_07]
Q1. A natural element becomes a resource only when humans learn how to use it with _, __ and technology.

✅ Answer: A natural element becomes a resource only when humans learn how to use it with knowledge, skills and technology.
Q2. The usefulness of a natural resource depends on three key factors — accessibility, _, and cultural acceptance.
✅ Answer: The usefulness of a natural resource depends on three key factors — accessibility, feasibility, and cultural acceptance.
Q3. The Treaty of _ (1665) forced Shivaji to surrender several forts to the Mughals but helped him safeguard the core of his kingdom.
✅ Answer: The Treaty of Purandar (1665) forced Shivaji to surrender several forts to the Mughals but helped him safeguard the core of his kingdom.
Q4. In 1674, Shivaji was crowned as Chhatrapati at _ Fort, marking the formal establishment of the Maratha Empire.
✅ Answer: In 1674, Shivaji was crowned as Chhatrapati at Raigad Fort, marking the formal establishment of the Maratha Empire.
Q5. The Age of Exploration began in the _ century when European powers like Spain, Portugal, and Britain started expanding across Asia, Africa, and the Americas.
✅ Answer: The Age of Exploration began in the 15th century when European powers like Spain, Portugal, and Britain started expanding across Asia, Africa, and the Americas.
Q6. The main causes of colonial expansion were political rivalry, economic greed, religious motives, and the desire for _ and exploration.
✅ Answer: The main causes of colonial expansion were political rivalry, economic greed, religious motives, and the desire for scientific inquiry and exploration.
Q7. The Battle of _ in 1757 marked the turning point when the British East India Company gained control over Bengal.
✅ Answer: The Battle of Plassey in 1757 marked the turning point when the British East India Company gained control over Bengal.
Q8. The Doctrine of _, introduced by Lord Dalhousie, allowed the British to annex any Indian state whose ruler died without a natural male heir.
✅ Answer: The Doctrine of Lapse, introduced by Lord Dalhousie, allowed the British to annex any Indian state whose ruler died without a natural male heir.
Q9. The voting process in India uses Electronic Voting Machines (EVMs) along with _ slips to ensure transparency and voter verification.
✅ Answer: The voting process in India uses Electronic Voting Machines (EVMs) along with VVPAT (Voter Verifiable Paper Audit Trail) slips to ensure transparency and voter verification.
Q10. The _ Code of Conduct is a set of rules created by the Election Commission to maintain fairness and prevent misuse of power during elections.
✅ Answer: The Model Code of Conduct is a set of rules created by the Election Commission to maintain fairness and prevent misuse of power during elections.
Q11. Members of the Rajya Sabha are elected indirectly by the MLAs of State Legislative Assemblies through the Single Transferable Vote (STV) system.
✅ Answer: True
Q12. Production is the process of using various inputs such as land, labour, capital and technology to create _ and services that meet human needs.
✅ Answer: Production is the process of using various inputs such as land, labour, capital and technology to create goods and services that meet human needs.
Q13. The natural resources like soil, forests, water and minerals together form the factor of production known as _.
✅ Answer: The natural resources like soil, forests, water and minerals together form the factor of production known as land.
Q14. Human effort, whether physical or mental, used in the process of production is known as _.
✅ Answer: Human effort, whether physical or mental, used in the process of production is known as labour.
Q15. The knowledge, skills, education and health of people that make them more efficient and productive are called __ capital.
✅ Answer: The knowledge, skills, education and health of people that make them more efficient and productive are called human capital.
Q1. Human effort, skill, and technology play a major role in converting natural elements into resources.
✅ Answer: True
Q2. Water and soil are examples of non-renewable resources that cannot be regenerated.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Water and soil are examples of renewable resources that can be regenerated if used wisely.
Q3. Air and sunlight are considered ubiquitous resources because they are found almost everywhere.
✅ Answer: True
Q4. Individual resources belong equally to all citizens of a country.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Individual resources are privately owned by individuals for personal benefit, while national resources belong to the country under government control.
Q5. The Bhakti movement in Maharashtra helped create unity and confidence among the common people.
✅ Answer: True
Q6. Shivaji Maharaj's early inspirations came only from military leaders and not from saints or family.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Shivaji Maharaj's early inspirations came from his mother Jijabai, cultural traditions and Bhakti saints, along with military training.
Q7. Guerilla warfare used large open battles to defeat the enemy quickly.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Guerrilla warfare used small, swift, surprise attacks using knowledge of terrain to defeat stronger enemies.
Q8. Shivaji's daring escape from Agra showed his courage and intelligence.
✅ Answer: True
Q9. The Doctrine of Lapse didn't respect the Indian tradition of adoption for royal succession.
✅ Answer: True
Q10. The policy of Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers militarily stronger and independent.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: The policy of Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers weak, dependent and powerless under British control.
Q11. The Bengal Famine of 1770 was made worse by British policy of high taxation and cash crops.
✅ Answer: True
Q12. The Revolt of 1857 was limited only to Indian soldiers and did not involve civilians
✅ Answer: False
Correction: The Revolt of 1857 grew into a large-scale movement involving sepoys, peasants, artisans, landlords and rulers.
Q13. The minimum age to vote in India was always 18 years since independence.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: The minimum age to vote was 21 years since independence and was reduced to 18 years by the 61st Constitutional Amendment Act in 1988.
Q14. The Election Commission of India works under the control of the central government.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: The Election Commission of India is an independent constitutional body not under the direct control of the government.
Q15. The Chief Election Commissioner is the head of the Election Commission of India.
✅ Answer: True
Q16. Human capital improves through education, training and healthcare.
✅ Answer: True
Q17. Machines and money are examples of capital, which are man-made resources used in production.
✅ Answer: True
Q18. Entrepreneurship does not involve taking risks or introducing new ideas.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Entrepreneurship involves taking risks, innovating and organising other factors of production to start businesses.

True / False - Q1 to Q18

Q1. Human effort, skill, and technology play a major role in converting natural elements into resources.
Answer: True

Q2. Water and soil are examples of non-renewable resources that cannot be regenerated.
Answer: False
Correction: Water and soil are examples of renewable resources that can be regenerated if used wisely.

Q3. Air and sunlight are considered ubiquitous resources because they are found almost everywhere.
Answer: True

Q4. Individual resources belong equally to all citizens of a country.
Answer: False
Correction: Individual resources are privately owned by people like land, houses, wells, vehicles; resources belonging to the country under government control are national resources.

Q5. The Bhakti movement in Maharashtra helped create unity and confidence among the common people.
Answer: True

Q6. Shivaji Maharaj's early inspirations came only from military leaders and not from saints or family.
Answer: False
Correction: Shivaji was mainly inspired by his mother Jijabai's teachings of Ramayana-Mahabharata, Bhakti saints like Dnyaneshwar, Tukaram, Ramdas, and military training.

Q7. Guerilla warfare used large open battles to defeat the enemy quickly.
Answer: False
Correction: Guerrilla warfare used speed, surprise, small swift attacks and knowledge of Sahyadri terrain to defeat stronger enemies, not large open battles.

Q8. Shivaji's daring escape from Agra showed his courage and intelligence.
Answer: True

Q9. The Doctrine of Lapse didn't respect the Indian tradition of adoption for royal succession.
Answer: True

Q10. The policy of Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers militarily stronger and independent.
Answer: False
Correction: The Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers weak, dependent and powerless under British Residents, reducing their sovereignty.

Q11. The Bengal Famine of 1770 was made worse by British policy of high taxation and cash crops.
Answer: True

Q12. The Revolt of 1857 was limited only to Indian soldiers and did not involve civilians
Answer: False
Correction: The Revolt of 1857 began with sepoys but grew into a large-scale movement involving peasants, artisans, landlords and rulers like Rani Lakshmibai, Nana Saheb, Begum Hazrat Mahal.

Q13. The minimum age to vote in India was always 18 years since independence.
Answer: False
Correction: The voting age was 21 years at independence and was reduced from 21 to 18 years by the 61st Constitutional Amendment Act, 1988.

Q14. The Election Commission of India works under the control of the central government.
Answer: False
Correction: The Election Commission of India is an independent constitutional body under Article 324, not under direct control of the government.

Q15. The Chief Election Commissioner is the head of the Election Commission of India.
Answer: True

Q16. Human capital improves through education, training and healthcare.
Answer: True

Q17. Machines and money are examples of capital, which are man-made resources used in production.
Answer: True

Q18. Entrepreneurship does not involve taking risks or introducing new ideas.
Answer: False
Correction: Entrepreneurship involves taking risks, innovating and combining land, labour, capital and technology to start new businesses.

B. Understanding Based Questions

Q1. The ability to manage and protect natural resources for future generations. [Chapter_01]
