D. HOTS (Higher Order Thinking Skills)

Q1. Suppose a village depends entirely on groundwater for farming. Using the example of Punjab, explain what advice you would give to prevent a future water crisis. [Chapter_01]
Q2. The Marathas built forts on hilltops and coasts instead of open plains. What does this reveal about their understanding of defense and survival? [Chapter_03]
Q3. The building of railways and telegraphs was often called a 'gift of modernisation'. Do you think these developments were truly meant for India's progress or mainly for strengthening British control? Give reasons. [Chapter_04]
Q4. Imagine the voting age in India had never been reduced from 21 to 18 years. How might this have changed youth participation in politics and the overall energy and direction of India's democracy? [Chapter_05]
Q5. Suppose a country invests heavily in machines but ignores education and training. How might this imbalance affect production and overall development in the long run? [Chapter_07]
Q1. Suppose a village depends entirely on groundwater for farming. Using the example of Punjab, explain what advice you would give to prevent a future water crisis.

✅ Answer:
Claim: The village must use groundwater wisely through stewardship, conservation and sustainable farming, otherwise it will face Punjab-like depletion and pollution crisis.
Evidence: In Punjab, the 'Granary of India,' farmers shifted to high-yielding varieties in the 1960s and 1970s which required far more water and began pumping groundwater extensively through tube wells; free electricity encouraged over-pumping while heavy use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides polluted underground water; water table fell to alarming levels, in some areas more than 30 metres deep, almost 80% of state's area is classified as 'over-exploited,' wells dried up, irrigation became difficult and polluted water created health hazards, soil degradation and unsafe drinking water.
Reasoning: Overexploitation happens when humans use resources faster than nature's ability to replace them; groundwater naturally refills through rainfall and underground recharge only if given time and space to heal; continuous pumping lowers water table and excessive chemicals damage soil and water, so restoration needs rainwater harvesting, crop rotation, organic manure and compost, and avoiding chemical-only farming as shown by Sikkim becoming 100% organic in 2016 with compost, neem and garlic repellents and variety of crops.
Final: Regulate pumping, harvest rainwater, adopt organic methods and crop diversity to prevent scarcity.

Q2. The Marathas built forts on hilltops and coasts instead of open plains. What does this reveal about their understanding of defense and survival?

✅ Answer:
Claim: It reveals a strategic understanding that forts are the lifeline of power, using geography for defence, trade control, administration and survival.
Evidence: Starting with Torna Fort in 1645 at age sixteen, Shivaji captured Purandar, Rajgad and Kondana later Sinhagad located on hilltops or near major routes which made them easy to defend and useful for controlling trade and movement; forts stored food, weapons and money and served as safe shelters and administrative centres; sea forts Sindhudurg, Vijaydurg and Suvarnadurg along Konkan coast were equipped with cannons and secret escape routes, making them almost impossible to capture; forts had strong stone walls, secret passages and clever designs; Ramchandrapant Amatya said without forts, the kingdom would not survive.
Reasoning: Growing up in rugged Sahyadri hills, Shivaji developed guerrilla warfare — striking suddenly and escaping into hills or forests — which gave advantage over much larger Mughal armies; hill and sea forts allowed watching enemy movements, sending troops quickly, protecting fishermen, traders, villages from pirates and foreign powers, and protecting trade routes.
Final: Hilltop and coastal forts show mastery of terrain-based defence and sustainable survival.

Q3. The building of railways and telegraphs was often called a 'gift of modernisation'. Do you think these developments were truly meant for India's progress or mainly for strengthening British control? Give reasons.

✅ Answer:
Claim: Railways and telegraphs were mainly tools of British economic and political control, not for India's welfare.
Evidence: Railway network was mainly designed to transport raw materials such as cotton, coal, jute, indigo and food grains from interior to major ports like Bombay, Calcutta and Madras to feed British factories, and to flood Indian markets with cheap machine-made British goods which ruined local industries; telegraph gave ability to communicate quickly during wars, revolts or famines; railways moved troops, weapons and supplies quickly to defeat rebellions such as the Revolt of 1857; construction was paid from Indian tax revenues with guaranteed profits to companies, with losses covered by Indian taxpayers.
Reasoning: Colonial impact shows limited benefits served colonisers; routes ignored traditional trade centres and linked resource-rich areas to ports; administration, bureaucracy, military and luxurious lifestyles of officials were financed through Indian money instead of schools, hospitals or irrigation.
Final: Railways and telegraphs strengthened British exploitation and rule.

Q4. Imagine the voting age in India had never been reduced from 21 to 18 years. How might this have changed youth participation in politics and the overall energy and direction of India's democracy?

✅ Answer:
Claim: Keeping voting age at 21 would have excluded millions of youth, weakening participation, equality and democratic energy.
Evidence: Constitution allowed vote at 21 years and above; 61st Constitutional Amendment Act in 1988 reduced voting age from 21 to 18 years, applied from 1989 general elections onward; this added over 35 million new young voters; leaders recognised young people were more aware, educated and energetic, contributing in education, jobs, agriculture and politics; universal adult franchise ensures Voice in Governance, Encourages Participation, Strengthens Unity and Trust, and makes government 'of the people, by the people and for the people.'
Reasoning: Universal franchise is foundation of democracy giving equal voice regardless of wealth, gender, caste or education; youth are large population and future of nation; without them, decisions would be made by only part of population, reducing accountability of leaders, civic awareness and trust in institutions.
Final: Without age reduction, India would be less inclusive, less youthful and less strong democratically.

Q5. Suppose a country invests heavily in machines but ignores education and training. How might this imbalance affect production and overall development in the long run?

✅ Answer:
Claim: Machines alone cannot produce; ignoring human capital will make production weak, inefficient and unsustainable.
Evidence: Labour is the most active factor of production because it transforms resources into goods; human capital is knowledge, skills, education, experience and health; World Bank notes human capital contributes nearly 60% of country's wealth; Japan and South Korea with few natural resources became leaders by investing in education, innovation and skill development; capital-intensive industries still need skilled engineers, scientists and technicians to design, monitor and repair machines; during COVID-19 lockdown in 2020, clothing factories with land, cotton and machines stopped because labour migrated, proving interdependence; challenges like poor quality of education, skill mismatch, and limited access to technology leave youth unemployed or underemployed.
Reasoning: All factors — land, labour, capital, entrepreneurship and technology — must work in harmony; fertile land without seeds or workers without tools cannot produce; entrepreneurs combine factors but need trained, healthy workers for efficiency, quality, less wastage and innovation.
Final: Neglecting education creates skill gaps, low productivity and stalled development despite machines.
Q1. Suppose a village depends entirely on groundwater for farming. Using the example of Punjab, explain what advice you would give to prevent a future water crisis.

Answer:
Step 1: Recall Punjab case - known as 'Granary of India', success in Green Revolution with high-yielding varieties in 1960s-1970s required far more water.
Step 2: Recall cause of crisis - extensive pumping through tube wells, free electricity encouraged over-pumping, heavy use of chemical fertilisers and pesticides polluted underground water.
Step 3: Recall consequence - water table fallen to more than 30 metres deep, almost 80% area classified as 'over-exploited', wells dried up, irrigation difficult, soil degradation and unsafe drinking water and health hazards.
Step 4: Give advice for stewardship and judicious use:
- Do not pump faster than natural recharge through rainfall and underground recharge; regulate tube wells.
- Practise rainwater harvesting and use drip irrigation like Israel model to grow crops with minimal water.
- Follow restoration - keep soil healthy with crop rotation, organic manure and compost instead of only chemical fertilisers, as in Sikkim organic farming model.
- Avoid growing only high-water crops; choose traditional / seasonal crops and diversify.
- Keep water sources clean and pollution-free, do not dump waste and chemicals.

Final: To prevent a Punjab-like crisis, the village must use groundwater wisely, allow nature time to refill, harvest rainwater, use drip irrigation, practise organic farming and crop rotation, and avoid over-pumping and chemical pollution for sustainable development.

Q2. The Marathas built forts on hilltops and coasts instead of open plains. What does this reveal about their understanding of defense and survival?

Answer:
Step 1: Recall examples - Torna, Purandar, Rajgad, Kondana / Sinhagad on hilltops or near major routes; sea forts Sindhudurg, Vijaydurg and Suvarnadurg along Konkan coast.
Step 2: Recall military advantage - hilltop location made them easy to defend and difficult to capture with strong stone walls, secret passages, secret water tanks and clever designs; sea forts had cannons and secret escape routes.
Step 3: Recall strategic use - control over trade and movement, watch enemy movements, send troops quickly, store food, weapons and money, safe shelters for soldiers and local people during war.
Step 4: Recall link to guerrilla warfare - small groups using knowledge of Sahyadri hills, forests, valleys and hill passes to attack suddenly and escape, advantage over much larger Mughal and Bijapur armies.
Step 5: Conclude understanding - as Ramchandrapant Amatya said, without forts kingdom would not survive; forts were lifeline and administrative centres, not just military bases.

Final: Building forts on hilltops and coasts shows Marathas understood geography as defence - using terrain, height, sea control and secret design for security, trade control, food storage, people's safety and guerrilla survival against stronger enemies.

Q3. The building of railways and telegraphs was often called a 'gift of modernisation'. Do you think these developments were truly meant for India's progress or mainly for strengthening British control? Give reasons.

Answer:
Step 1: State position - mainly for strengthening British economic and political control, not for people's welfare; they were tools of control rather than instruments of welfare.
Step 2: Economic reason - railways designed to transport raw materials like cotton, coal, jute, indigo and food grains from interior to ports like Bombay, Calcutta and Madras for export to feed British factories, and to flood Indian markets with cheap machine-made British goods which ruined local industries.
Step 3: Political-military reason - telegraph gave ability to communicate quickly during wars, revolts or famines; railways moved troops, weapons and supplies quickly to defeat rebellions such as the Revolt of 1857; traditional trade centres and routes were ignored.
Step 4: Financial reason - expenses taken from Indian tax revenues, railway companies guaranteed profits but losses covered by Indian taxpayers; Indians were made to pay for their own subjugation instead of investment in schools, hospitals or irrigation.
Step 5: Accept limited benefits - distant regions connected, markets linked, faster travel for ordinary people, but routes were not built to serve Indian needs.

Final: Railways and telegraphs were mainly meant for British control - for resource extraction, market flooding, troop movement and quick communication - with only limited side benefits for Indians, paid for by Indians themselves.

Q4. Imagine the voting age in India had never been reduced from 21 to 18 years. How might this have changed youth participation in politics and the overall energy and direction of India's democracy?

Answer:
Step 1: Recall history - Constitution allowed vote at 21 years; 61st Constitutional Amendment Act in 1988 reduced voting age from 21 to 18 years, applied from 1989 general elections.
Step 2: Recall effect - added over 35 million new young voters, youth became most powerful section; recognised youth were more aware, educated and energetic, contributing in education, jobs, agriculture and politics.
Step 3: Imagine without change - millions of 18-21 year olds excluded, large youth population of India left without direct role in decision-making.
Step 4: Impact on participation - lower voter turnout, less political awareness and sense of responsibility among youth, weaker voice in governance and less representation of youth concerns.
Step 5: Impact on democracy - less energy, creativity and inclusive growth; principle of universal adult franchise - equality, voice in governance, foundation of 'government of the people, by the people and for the people', accountability and unity - would be weakened.

Final: Without reduction to 18 years, youth participation would fall, democracy would be less representative, less energetic and less strong, as the country's large youth power would not get equal voice in choosing leaders and shaping the nation's future.

Q5. Suppose a country invests heavily in machines but ignores education and training. How might this imbalance affect production and overall development in the long run?

Answer:
Step 1: Recall interdependence - production is successful only when land, labour, capital, entrepreneurship and technology work together; if one factor is missing, entire system becomes weak, as shown by COVID-19 lockdown when factories with land and machines stopped without labour.
Step 2: Recall role of human capital - knowledge, skills, education, experience and health make people productive; education and training are backbone, provide specialised abilities and innovation.
Step 3: Recall need for experts even in capital-intensive production - automobile factories, semiconductor plants need skilled engineers, scientists and technicians to design, supervise, monitor and repair machines; without human capital machines remain idle.
Step 4: Effect of imbalance - skill mismatch, unemployment or underemployment, low efficiency and poor quality, more wastage, no creativity or innovation, machines break down or misused.
Step 5: Long-run development effect - strong human capital base is essential for economic growth; ignoring it wastes demographic dividend, slows growth, increases dependence, harms sustainability and social progress.

Final: Investing only in machines without education and training will weaken production - machines cannot run alone, efficiency and quality will fall, unemployment will rise, innovation will stop, and long-term economic growth and development will suffer.

E. Analysis Based Questions

Q1. Countries like Japan prosper despite limited natural resources, while some resource-rich nations remain poor. Analyse what this tells us about the real factors behind a nation's development. [Chapter_01]
Q2. Shivaji respected both Hindu temples and Muslim saints. What message does this give about unity and respect in a diverse country like India? [Chapter_03]
Q3. Analyse how the Subsidiary Alliance System forced Indian rulers to depend on the British. How did this weaken India's political independence? [Chapter_04]
Q4. Imagine if India still followed the old system where only the wealthy or educated could vote. Analyse how it would change the social and political structure of the country today? [Chapter_05]
Q5. A new startup hires unskilled workers and trains them to make solar lamps. Explain how this business uses both human capital and technology to promote sustainable development and reduce unemployment. [Chapter_07]
Q1. Countries like Japan prosper despite limited natural resources, while some resource-rich nations remain poor. Analyse what this tells us about the real factors behind a nation's development?

✅ Answer:
Claim: Development does not depend only on having abundant natural resources, but on how wisely a country uses its wealth and builds its human capital, technology and governance.
Evidence: Japan does not have large reserves of coal, oil or minerals, yet it has become one of the richest and most advanced countries by investing in technology, innovation, skilled workers and international trade. In contrast, African countries such as Nigeria and the Democratic Republic of Congo have abundant reserves of oil, diamonds and minerals, yet many people there still face poverty, poor health facilities and lack of education due to corruption and misuse of wealth, inequality, overdependence on resources, and continuous conflicts.
Reasoning: This surprising situation is known as the 'Resource Curse' or the 'Paradox of Plenty' — when wealth from resources does not benefit common people but creates economic, political and social problems, while wise use, diversification into farming, industries and technology, and peace lead to progress.
Final: The real factors behind development are good governance, education, innovation, diversification and peaceful use of resources, not resource richness alone.

Q2. Shivaji respected both Hindu temples and Muslim saints. What message does this give about unity and respect in a diverse country like India?

✅ Answer:
Claim: It gives the message of religious tolerance, harmony and unity in diversity as the basis of just rule and national identity.
Evidence: Shivaji is remembered for protecting Hindu temples, rebuilding those destroyed during wars and also respecting Muslim saints and dargahs. His policies of religious tolerance, justice and protection of ordinary people made him a beloved ruler among Hindus, Muslims and other communities. The Marathas encouraged religious harmony and supported people of different faiths, which allowed literature, music and painting to flourish.
Reasoning: By honouring all faiths and protecting people from exploitation, Shivaji created trust between people and rulers and gave people a strong sense of identity and pride, showing that respecting diversity strengthens unity.
Final: In a diverse country like India, respecting all religions promotes peace, cooperation and shared pride, as shown by Shivaji's example.

Q3. Analyse how the Subsidiary Alliance System forced Indian rulers to depend on the British. How did this weaken India's political independence?

✅ Answer:
Claim: The Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers militarily, financially and politically dependent, reducing their sovereignty to a mere show.
Evidence: Introduced by Lord Wellesley around 1798, Indian rulers had to agree to keep British troops stationed in their states 'for protection' and pay the heavy cost of maintaining them, give up the right to have their own independent armies or make alliances with other powers, and accept a British Resident in their courts who guided or controlled most decisions. The ruler of Hyderabad was first to accept in 1798, followed by Awadh and Mysore, and once a state entered it could never leave without facing a powerful British military response — an 'empire on the cheap'.
Reasoning: Loss of independent army plus financial burden plus Resident control meant rulers looked independent but were completely dependent, could not unite or resist, allowing the British to dominate most of India without direct rule.
Final: The system weakened political independence by destroying military self-reliance, draining revenue, and transferring real decision-making to the British.

Q4. Imagine if India still followed the old system where only the wealthy or educated could vote. Analyse how it would change the social and political structure of the country today?

✅ Answer:
Claim: It would destroy political equality and return India to discrimination, elite domination and weak democracy.
Evidence: Before independence, only about 13% of Indians were allowed to vote — mostly wealthy people, landlords, or those with high education and property, while ordinary citizens, the poor and especially women had no say. In 1950 the Constitution granted universal adult franchise to every citizen above 18 years irrespective of caste, religion, gender, language, education, wealth or region, ensuring equality, voice in governance, representation of women, Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes, minorities, prevention of discrimination, and accountability of leaders.
Reasoning: Without universal franchise, government would be formed by a small privileged group, not the will of the majority; concerns of weaker sections would be ignored, domination by one group would grow, leaders would not be accountable, and unity and trust in institutions would fall, violating 'government of the people, by the people and for the people'.
Final: Continuing the old system would create an unequal, exclusionary political structure where the poor and marginalized are powerless and democracy loses its meaning.

Q5. A new startup hires unskilled workers and trains them to make solar lamps. Explain how this business uses both human capital and technology to promote sustainable development and reduce unemployment.

✅ Answer:
Claim: The startup builds human capital through training and uses clean technology to create jobs and sustainable production.
Evidence: Human capital refers to the knowledge, skills, health and abilities that make people more productive; education and training are its backbone, and investment in schools, training and healthcare improves efficiency and creativity. Technology is the application of scientific knowledge, tools and modern methods to make production faster, more accurate and cost-effective, such as solar energy as a clean, unlimited renewable source. Entrepreneurs create businesses and generate employment for workers, engineers and staff, while responsibilities include fair wages, safe conditions, worker training, conservation, pollution control and sustainability for the future.
Reasoning: Training unskilled workers improves their skill and productivity, giving them income and reducing unemployment, while making solar lamps uses renewable energy to reduce dependence on coal and petroleum, lower pollution, and meet today's needs without harming future generations.
Final: By combining skilled human capital with solar technology, the startup promotes inclusive growth, clean energy and long-term sustainable development.

Q1. Countries like Japan prosper despite limited natural resources, while some resource-rich nations remain poor. Analyse what this tells us about the real factors behind a nation's development.

Steps:
1. Chapter explains this surprising situation as the 'Resource Curse' or 'Paradox of Plenty' - wealth from natural resources does not automatically make a country wealthy and developed.
2. Resource-rich countries like Nigeria and Democratic Republic of Congo have abundant oil, diamonds and minerals, yet face poverty, poor health and lack of education because of - corruption and misuse of wealth, inequality where only few benefit, overdependence on one resource, and continuous conflicts and political instability.
3. In contrast, Japan has very limited coal, oil or minerals, yet became one of the richest and most advanced countries by investing in technology, innovation, skilled workers, human capital and international trade.
4. This proves development depends not only on having resources, but on how wisely a country uses them - good governance, education and health, diversified economy, fair sharing, and peace.

Final: A nation's development is decided by wise use, human capital, technology, honesty and unity, not merely by abundance of natural resources.

Q2. Shivaji respected both Hindu temples and Muslim saints. What message does this give about unity and respect in a diverse country like India?

Steps:
1. Shivaji built his Swarajya on justice, equality, independence and dharma, with fair justice and protection for ordinary people, farmers, women and citizens during wars.
2. He is remembered for protecting and rebuilding Hindu temples destroyed during wars, and also respecting Muslim saints and dargahs, encouraging religious harmony and tolerance.
3. This atmosphere of tolerance allowed literature, music, painting and Marathi culture to flourish and made him beloved among Hindus, Muslims and other communities under his rule.
4. For diverse India, it gives the message that rulers and citizens must respect all faiths, protect cultural heritage, and treat everyone equally to build trust, unity and pride.

Final: Shivaji's religious tolerance teaches that unity in diversity, mutual respect and justice for all strengthen the nation and keep the spirit of Swarajya alive.

Q3. Analyse how the Subsidiary Alliance System forced Indian rulers to depend on the British. How did this weaken India's political independence?

Steps:
1. The Subsidiary Alliance, introduced by Lord Wellesley around 1798, required Indian rulers to keep British troops stationed in their states 'for protection' and pay the heavy cost of maintaining them.
2. Rulers had to give up their own independent armies and the right to make alliances with other powers, and accept a British Resident in their court who guided or controlled most decisions.
3. The ruler of Hyderabad was first to accept in 1798, followed by Awadh, Mysore and others. Once joined, a state could never leave without facing powerful British military response.
4. On paper rulers looked independent, but in reality they became weak, powerless and dependent - sovereignty reduced to mere show. For British it was an 'empire on the cheap' - rulers bore costs while British enjoyed power, preventing united resistance and laying foundations for nearly two centuries of colonial rule.

Final: The Subsidiary Alliance destroyed military, financial and political independence of Indian states and made them dependent puppets of the British.

Q4. Imagine if India still followed the old system where only the wealthy or educated could vote. Analyse how it would change the social and political structure of the country today?

Steps:
1. Before independence, only about 13% Indians - mostly wealthy, landlords, high education and property holders - could vote, while poor and especially women had no say.
2. The Constitution in 1950 introduced universal adult franchise - every citizen 18 years and above, irrespective of caste, religion, gender, language, education or wealth, with equal value of vote.
3. If old system continued, government would be of privileged few, not 'of the people, by the people and for the people' - women, Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes, minorities, poor farmers and labourers would have no representation and face discrimination and domination by one group.
4. Leaders would not be accountable to the majority, participation and awareness would fall, social divisions would increase, and unity, trust and inclusive growth would weaken, destroying the foundation of democracy.

Final: Without universal adult franchise, India would be unequal, unjust and undemocratic, ruled by a small elite instead of the will of all citizens.

Q5. A new startup hires unskilled workers and trains them to make solar lamps. Explain how this business uses both human capital and technology to promote sustainable development and reduce unemployment.

Steps:
1. Human capital is knowledge, skills, education, health and abilities that make people productive. By hiring unskilled workers and training them, the startup builds human capital through education, training and skill development, making them efficient, creative and employable.
2. This creates entrepreneurship roles of business creation and job generation - chefs, workers, technicians get jobs, incomes rise, unemployment falls, and dignity of labour is respected with fair wages and safe conditions.
3. Technology is application of scientific knowledge to make production faster and efficient. Solar lamps use sunlight - an unlimited, clean, renewable resource - through solar panels, reducing dependence on coal, petroleum and electricity, and reducing pollution.
4. Together they ensure sustainable production - meeting today's needs without harming future generations - by combining environmental responsibility like conservation, pollution control and renewable energy, with social responsibility like training, worker welfare and community development.

Final: The startup turns unskilled labour into skilled human capital and uses clean solar technology to provide jobs, income and eco-friendly light, promoting inclusive growth and sustainable development.

Q1. A natural element becomes a resource only when humans learn how to use it with _, __ and technology.

✅ Answer: A natural element becomes a resource only when humans learn how to use it with knowledge, skills and technology.
Q2. The usefulness of a natural resource depends on three key factors — accessibility, _, and cultural acceptance.
✅ Answer: The usefulness of a natural resource depends on three key factors — accessibility, feasibility, and cultural acceptance.
Q3. The Treaty of _ (1665) forced Shivaji to surrender several forts to the Mughals but helped him safeguard the core of his kingdom.
✅ Answer: The Treaty of Purandar (1665) forced Shivaji to surrender several forts to the Mughals but helped him safeguard the core of his kingdom.
Q4. In 1674, Shivaji was crowned as Chhatrapati at _ Fort, marking the formal establishment of the Maratha Empire.
✅ Answer: In 1674, Shivaji was crowned as Chhatrapati at Raigad Fort, marking the formal establishment of the Maratha Empire.
Q5. The Age of Exploration began in the _ century when European powers like Spain, Portugal, and Britain started expanding across Asia, Africa, and the Americas.
✅ Answer: The Age of Exploration began in the 15th century when European powers like Spain, Portugal, and Britain started expanding across Asia, Africa, and the Americas.
Q6. The main causes of colonial expansion were political rivalry, economic greed, religious motives, and the desire for _ and exploration.
✅ Answer: The main causes of colonial expansion were political rivalry, economic greed, religious motives, and the desire for scientific inquiry and exploration.
Q7. The Battle of _ in 1757 marked the turning point when the British East India Company gained control over Bengal.
✅ Answer: The Battle of Plassey in 1757 marked the turning point when the British East India Company gained control over Bengal.
Q8. The Doctrine of _, introduced by Lord Dalhousie, allowed the British to annex any Indian state whose ruler died without a natural male heir.
✅ Answer: The Doctrine of Lapse, introduced by Lord Dalhousie, allowed the British to annex any Indian state whose ruler died without a natural male heir.
Q9. The voting process in India uses Electronic Voting Machines (EVMs) along with _ slips to ensure transparency and voter verification.
✅ Answer: The voting process in India uses Electronic Voting Machines (EVMs) along with VVPAT (Voter Verifiable Paper Audit Trail) slips to ensure transparency and voter verification.
Q10. The _ Code of Conduct is a set of rules created by the Election Commission to maintain fairness and prevent misuse of power during elections.
✅ Answer: The Model Code of Conduct is a set of rules created by the Election Commission to maintain fairness and prevent misuse of power during elections.
Q11. Members of the Rajya Sabha are elected indirectly by the MLAs of State Legislative Assemblies through the Single Transferable Vote (STV) system.
✅ Answer: True
Q12. Production is the process of using various inputs such as land, labour, capital and technology to create _ and services that meet human needs.
✅ Answer: Production is the process of using various inputs such as land, labour, capital and technology to create goods and services that meet human needs.
Q13. The natural resources like soil, forests, water and minerals together form the factor of production known as _.
✅ Answer: The natural resources like soil, forests, water and minerals together form the factor of production known as land.
Q14. Human effort, whether physical or mental, used in the process of production is known as _.
✅ Answer: Human effort, whether physical or mental, used in the process of production is known as labour.
Q15. The knowledge, skills, education and health of people that make them more efficient and productive are called __ capital.
✅ Answer: The knowledge, skills, education and health of people that make them more efficient and productive are called human capital.
Q1. Human effort, skill, and technology play a major role in converting natural elements into resources.
✅ Answer: True
Q2. Water and soil are examples of non-renewable resources that cannot be regenerated.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Water and soil are examples of renewable resources that can be regenerated if used wisely.
Q3. Air and sunlight are considered ubiquitous resources because they are found almost everywhere.
✅ Answer: True
Q4. Individual resources belong equally to all citizens of a country.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Individual resources are privately owned by individuals for personal benefit, while national resources belong to the country under government control.
Q5. The Bhakti movement in Maharashtra helped create unity and confidence among the common people.
✅ Answer: True
Q6. Shivaji Maharaj's early inspirations came only from military leaders and not from saints or family.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Shivaji Maharaj's early inspirations came from his mother Jijabai, cultural traditions and Bhakti saints, along with military training.
Q7. Guerilla warfare used large open battles to defeat the enemy quickly.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Guerrilla warfare used small, swift, surprise attacks using knowledge of terrain to defeat stronger enemies.
Q8. Shivaji's daring escape from Agra showed his courage and intelligence.
✅ Answer: True
Q9. The Doctrine of Lapse didn't respect the Indian tradition of adoption for royal succession.
✅ Answer: True
Q10. The policy of Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers militarily stronger and independent.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: The policy of Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers weak, dependent and powerless under British control.
Q11. The Bengal Famine of 1770 was made worse by British policy of high taxation and cash crops.
✅ Answer: True
Q12. The Revolt of 1857 was limited only to Indian soldiers and did not involve civilians
✅ Answer: False
Correction: The Revolt of 1857 grew into a large-scale movement involving sepoys, peasants, artisans, landlords and rulers.
Q13. The minimum age to vote in India was always 18 years since independence.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: The minimum age to vote was 21 years since independence and was reduced to 18 years by the 61st Constitutional Amendment Act in 1988.
Q14. The Election Commission of India works under the control of the central government.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: The Election Commission of India is an independent constitutional body not under the direct control of the government.
Q15. The Chief Election Commissioner is the head of the Election Commission of India.
✅ Answer: True
Q16. Human capital improves through education, training and healthcare.
✅ Answer: True
Q17. Machines and money are examples of capital, which are man-made resources used in production.
✅ Answer: True
Q18. Entrepreneurship does not involve taking risks or introducing new ideas.
✅ Answer: False
Correction: Entrepreneurship involves taking risks, innovating and organising other factors of production to start businesses.

True / False - Q1 to Q18

Q1. Human effort, skill, and technology play a major role in converting natural elements into resources.
Answer: True

Q2. Water and soil are examples of non-renewable resources that cannot be regenerated.
Answer: False
Correction: Water and soil are examples of renewable resources that can be regenerated if used wisely.

Q3. Air and sunlight are considered ubiquitous resources because they are found almost everywhere.
Answer: True

Q4. Individual resources belong equally to all citizens of a country.
Answer: False
Correction: Individual resources are privately owned by people like land, houses, wells, vehicles; resources belonging to the country under government control are national resources.

Q5. The Bhakti movement in Maharashtra helped create unity and confidence among the common people.
Answer: True

Q6. Shivaji Maharaj's early inspirations came only from military leaders and not from saints or family.
Answer: False
Correction: Shivaji was mainly inspired by his mother Jijabai's teachings of Ramayana-Mahabharata, Bhakti saints like Dnyaneshwar, Tukaram, Ramdas, and military training.

Q7. Guerilla warfare used large open battles to defeat the enemy quickly.
Answer: False
Correction: Guerrilla warfare used speed, surprise, small swift attacks and knowledge of Sahyadri terrain to defeat stronger enemies, not large open battles.

Q8. Shivaji's daring escape from Agra showed his courage and intelligence.
Answer: True

Q9. The Doctrine of Lapse didn't respect the Indian tradition of adoption for royal succession.
Answer: True

Q10. The policy of Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers militarily stronger and independent.
Answer: False
Correction: The Subsidiary Alliance made Indian rulers weak, dependent and powerless under British Residents, reducing their sovereignty.

Q11. The Bengal Famine of 1770 was made worse by British policy of high taxation and cash crops.
Answer: True

Q12. The Revolt of 1857 was limited only to Indian soldiers and did not involve civilians
Answer: False
Correction: The Revolt of 1857 began with sepoys but grew into a large-scale movement involving peasants, artisans, landlords and rulers like Rani Lakshmibai, Nana Saheb, Begum Hazrat Mahal.

Q13. The minimum age to vote in India was always 18 years since independence.
Answer: False
Correction: The voting age was 21 years at independence and was reduced from 21 to 18 years by the 61st Constitutional Amendment Act, 1988.

Q14. The Election Commission of India works under the control of the central government.
Answer: False
Correction: The Election Commission of India is an independent constitutional body under Article 324, not under direct control of the government.

Q15. The Chief Election Commissioner is the head of the Election Commission of India.
Answer: True

Q16. Human capital improves through education, training and healthcare.
Answer: True

Q17. Machines and money are examples of capital, which are man-made resources used in production.
Answer: True

Q18. Entrepreneurship does not involve taking risks or introducing new ideas.
Answer: False
Correction: Entrepreneurship involves taking risks, innovating and combining land, labour, capital and technology to start new businesses.

III. Give one word for the following:

B. Understanding Based Questions

Q1. The ability to manage and protect natural resources for future generations. [Chapter_01]
Q2. The female ruler who led armies and defended Swarājya after Rājaram's death. [Chapter_03]

Q1. The ability to manage and protect natural resources for future generations.

✅ Answer:
Step 1: The chapter defines careful management of limited and precious resources as stewardship.
Step 2: It means acting as responsible caretakers of nature so resources are available for future generations.
Final: Stewardship

Q2. The female ruler who led armies and defended Swarājya after Rājaram's death.

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Tarabai, wife of Rajaram, emerged as a fearless warrior queen after the death of her husband.
Step 2: She personally took charge and led armies with guerrilla tactics to defend Maratha territories against Aurangzeb.
Final: Tarabai

Q3. The economic theory explaining how India's wealth was systematically taken to Britain.

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Dadabhai Naoroji in Poverty and Un-British Rule in India proved how India's riches were being drained to Britain.
Step 2: The chapter terms this systematic outflow of money, resources and profits as Drain of Wealth.
Final: Drain of Wealth

Q4. The system that gives every adult citizen an equal right to vote.

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Every citizen of India aged 18 or above has the legal right to vote irrespective of caste, religion, gender, wealth.
Step 2: The chapter defines this principle of political equality as universal adult franchise.
Final: Universal Adult Franchise

Q5. The combination of skill, education and health that increases human efficiency.

✅ Answer:
Step 1: The chapter defines the skills, knowledge, education, experience and health of people as a factor of production.
Step 2: It makes people more productive and efficient in work.
Final: Human Capital

I. Define the following:

Q1. Sustainable development [Chapter_01]
Q2. Swarājya [Chapter_03]
Q3. Doctrine of Lapse [Chapter_04]
Q4. Election Commission of India (ECI) [Chapter_05]
Q5. Corporate Social Responsibility [Chapter_07]
Q1. Sustainable development

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Sustainable development means using natural resources carefully so that we can meet our present needs without destroying or exhausting them for future generations.
Step 2: It is like borrowing resources from the Earth and returning them so that children and grandchildren can also enjoy them, by balancing economic growth, social welfare and environmental care.
Final: Careful use of resources for present needs without harming future generations.

Q2. Swarājya

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Swarājya is a Marathi term meaning 'self-rule', which was Shivaji Maharaj's dream and guiding principle of life and foundation of the Maratha Empire.
Step 2: It meant establishing a kingdom based on justice, equality and independence, where people could live freely, with justice and dignity, without oppression from foreign rulers like Mughals and Sultanates.
Final: Self-rule with justice, equality and independence free from foreign oppression.

Q3. Doctrine of Lapse

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Doctrine of Lapse was a British expansion policy introduced by Lord Dalhousie, Governor-General of India in the 19th century.
Step 2: Under it the British refused to recognise adopted heirs against Indian tradition, so if a ruler died without a natural male son, his state would be automatically annexed by the East India Company, as with Jhansi, Satara and Nagpur.
Final: British policy to annex states without a natural male heir by denying adoption.

Q4. Election Commission of India (ECI)

✅ Answer:
Step 1: The Election Commission of India was created in 1950 as an independent constitutional body under Article 324, not under direct control of the government to ensure neutral elections.
Step 2: It is responsible for conducting elections to the Lok Sabha, Rajya Sabha and State Legislative Assemblies, as well as to the offices of the President and Vice-President, acting as referee and watchdog of democracy.
Final: Independent constitutional body for conducting free, fair elections in India.

Q5. Corporate Social Responsibility

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Corporate Social Responsibility is the duty of companies to use part of their profits for the welfare of society.
Step 2: According to India's 2014 CSR law, certain companies must spend on community welfare projects like education, healthcare, sanitation, rural development, such as building schools, hospitals, planting trees.
Final: Duty of companies to spend part of profits for social welfare.
Q1. Sustainable development

  • Step 1: It means using natural resources carefully to meet our present needs.
  • Step 2: It means not destroying or exhausting them for future generations - like borrowing from Earth for our children and grandchildren.
  • Step 3: It requires balancing economic growth, social welfare and environmental care.
  • Step 4: It is practised in daily life through Respect and Care for all Forms of Life, improving quality of human life, conserving vitality and diversity, and following the Three Rs - Reduce, Reuse and Recycle.

Final: Sustainable development means using natural resources carefully so that we can meet our present needs without destroying or exhausting them for future generations, by balancing economic growth, social welfare and environmental care.

Q2. Swarājya

  • Step 1: It is a Marathi term meaning 'self-rule'.
  • Step 2: It was Shivaji Maharaj's dream and guiding principle of life.
  • Step 3: It aimed to establish a kingdom where people could live freely, with justice, equality, dignity and independence.
  • Step 4: It meant freedom from oppression by Mughals, Sultanates of Bijapur and Golconda and other foreign rulers, and became the foundation of the Maratha Empire.

Final: Swarājya means self-rule - Shivaji Maharaj's dream to establish an independent Maratha kingdom based on justice, equality and independence, where people could live with dignity and freedom without foreign oppression.

Q3. Doctrine of Lapse

  • Step 1: It was a British expansion policy introduced in the 19th century by Lord Dalhousie, Governor-General of India.
  • Step 2: Under Indian tradition, a ruler without natural male heir could adopt a son to inherit the throne.
  • Step 3: Under this Doctrine, the British refused to recognise adopted heirs.
  • Step 4: If a ruler died without a natural male son, his state would be automatically annexed by the East India Company, e.g., Jhansi, Satara and Nagpur.

Final: Doctrine of Lapse was Lord Dalhousie's policy by which the British refused to recognise adopted heirs and annexed any Indian state whose ruler died without a natural male heir.

Q4. Election Commission of India (ECI)

  • Step 1: It was created in 1950 as an independent constitutional body under Article 324 of the Constitution.
  • Step 2: Being independent means it is not under direct control of the government, ensuring neutral and unbiased elections.
  • Step 3: It is headed by the Chief Election Commissioner with two Election Commissioners, with CEOs, DEOs and ROs at state and district levels.
  • Step 4: It conducts elections to Lok Sabha, Rajya Sabha, State Legislative Assemblies, and President and Vice-President, declares dates, enforces Model Code of Conduct, registers parties, supervises polls with EVMs and VVPATs.

Final: The Election Commission of India is the independent constitutional body established in 1950 under Article 324, called the watchdog of Indian democracy, responsible for conducting free, fair and secure elections in India.

Q5. Corporate Social Responsibility

  • Step 1: It is the duty of companies to use part of their profits for welfare of society.
  • Step 2: Under India's 2014 CSR law, certain companies must spend a part of profits on community welfare projects.
  • Step 3: It includes Community Development, Education Support, Environmental Care and Public Welfare.
  • Step 4: Examples are building schools, providing scholarships, healthcare, sanitation, rural development, toilets, safe drinking water, medical camps, tree plantations and renewable energy.

Final: Corporate Social Responsibility is the duty of companies to spend a part of their profits for society's welfare such as education, healthcare, sanitation, rural development and environmental protection.

II. Differentiate between the following:

Q1. Renewable resources and Non-renewable resources [Chapter_01]
Q2. Bārgirs and Shiledārs [Chapter_03]
Q3. Portuguese and British methods of colonisation in India [Chapter_04]
Q4. Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha elections [Chapter_05]
Q5. Labour-intensive production and Capital-intensive production [Chapter_07]
Q1. Renewable resources and Non-renewable resources

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Renewable resources are those which can be replaced or restored by natural processes within a human lifetime. They are continuously available like sunlight and wind, and can regenerate like forests, water from rivers, soil, timber, geothermal energy and biomass if used wisely through afforestation, rainwater harvesting and organic farming. If exploited carelessly they may become scarce.
Step 2: Non-renewable resources are those that take millions of years to form in nature and cannot be quickly replaced once used up. They are stored deep inside the Earth in limited amounts like coal used in thermal plants, petroleum providing petrol-diesel-kerosene-LPG-plastics, natural gas, minerals and metals like iron-copper-gold, and nuclear energy from uranium and thorium. Their overuse causes scarcity, rising costs, conflicts and environmental harm through pollution, mining and greenhouse gases.
Final: Renewable resources regenerate in human lifetime and are eco-friendly if conserved; Non-renewable resources take millions of years to form, are limited, and get exhausted with pollution on overuse.

Q2. Bārgirs and Shiledārs

⚠️ Answer: Not covered in this chapter — NEVER guess or skip it.

Q3. Portuguese and British methods of colonisation in India

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Portuguese were the first Europeans to arrive by sea when Vasco da Gama reached Kappad near Kozhikode in May 1498. They captured strategic ports, most importantly Goa in 1510 as capital, built naval base, introduced cartaz system - compulsory pass for ships in Arabian Sea, monopolised spice trade for nearly a century, and tried to spread Christianity through Goa Inquisition established in 1560 with forced conversions and temple destruction.
Step 2: British East India Company came as traders in early 17th century under charter of Queen Elizabeth I to buy cotton textiles, silk, indigo and spices. They set up factories at Surat, Madras, Bombay, Calcutta, built forts around them, then turned from traders to rulers by Battle of Plassey in 1757 defeating Siraj-ud-Daulah with betrayal of Mir Jafar and Battle of Buxar in 1764 taking Diwani of Bengal, Bihar and Orissa, and expanded by Divide and Rule, Subsidiary Alliance introduced by Lord Wellesley around 1798, and Doctrine of Lapse introduced by Lord Dalhousie annexing Jhansi, Satara, Nagpur by refusing adopted heirs.
Final: Portuguese method was sea-based monopoly, cartaz passes and religious persecution from Goa; British method was trade-factories to forts to political control by battles, revenue rights and unfair policies like Divide and Rule, Subsidiary Alliance and Doctrine of Lapse.

Q4. Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha elections

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Lok Sabha and State Assemblies are directly elected by citizens above 18 years every five years. Country is divided into 543 constituencies for Lok Sabha, each elects one leader by First-Past-the-Post system where candidate with most votes wins. Elected members are called MPs and MLAs, majority party forms government with Prime Minister and Chief Minister.
Step 2: Rajya Sabha or Council of States is indirectly elected, not by citizens but by elected MLAs of State Assemblies. It has 245 members - 233 elected and 12 nominated by President for literature, science, art or social service. It is Permanent House never dissolved, one-third retire every two years, each serves six-year term, elected through Single Transferable Vote system with proportional representation to give voice to states.
Final: Lok Sabha is direct election by people through FPTP for 5 years; Rajya Sabha is indirect election by MLAs through STV, permanent body with 6-year term.

Q5. Labour-intensive production and Capital-intensive production

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Labour-intensive production is where human effort and skills play the most important role with less use of machines. Examples are agriculture, handicrafts, cottage industries and small shops. It depends on energy, strength, knowledge, creativity and patience of workers, uses simple tools and traditional techniques, takes more time and energy, and production is slower.
Step 2: Capital-intensive production is where machines, technology and large financial investments play the dominant role. Examples are automobile factories, semiconductor plants and space research centres. It needs huge money to build factories and buy modern machines, production is faster, cheaper per unit and more reliable with high efficiency, but still needs skilled engineers and technicians to design, monitor and repair.
Final: Labour-intensive depends mainly on manual skill and simple tools; Capital-intensive depends mainly on advanced machines, technology and large investment for bulk high-efficiency output.
Q1. Renewable resources and Non-renewable resources

Steps:
1. Meaning - Renewable: Resources which can be replaced or restored by natural processes within a human lifetime. They are continuously available in nature, but they are not unlimited - availability depends on careful use.
2. Meaning - Non-renewable: Resources that take millions of years to form in nature and cannot be quickly replaced once used up. They are stored deep inside the Earth and result of slow geological processes like decay of plants and animals, compression of minerals under heat and pressure.
3. Supply: Renewable - freely available like sunlight and wind never run out, while forests and soil can regenerate if used responsibly. Non-renewable - present only in limited amounts, once consumed cannot be replenished in our lifetime.
4. Examples - Renewable: Sunlight, Wind, Water from Rivers, Soil, Timber (wood), Geothermal Energy, Biomass.
5. Examples - Non-renewable: Coal, Petroleum (crude oil), Natural Gas, Minerals and Metals - Iron, copper, aluminium, gold, Nuclear Energy from uranium and thorium.
6. Effect of Overuse: Renewable - careless exploitation like cutting too many trees, polluting rivers, overfarming makes them scarce; causes deforestation, pollution threat, soil degradation. Non-renewable - overuse reduces supply and causes scarcity, energy crises, rising costs and conflicts; burning coal and petroleum releases greenhouse gases, global warming, mining damages land and forests.

Final:
| Renewable Resources | Non-Renewable Resources |
| :--- | :--- |
| Regenerate naturally within human lifetime if managed wisely | Take millions of years to form, cannot be replaced quickly |
| Continuous but limited if misused; need afforestation, rainwater harvesting, organic farming, solar/wind energy | Limited stock; need recycling of metals, energy-efficiency, shift to solar, wind, biogas |
| Eco-friendly energy, support survival and development | Foundation of industries, transport, electricity, daily fuels |

Q2. Bārgirs and Shiledārs

⚠️ Answer: Not covered in this chapter

Q3. Portuguese and British methods of colonisation in India

Steps:
1. Arrival and Aim - Portuguese: First Europeans to arrive in India by sea. Vasco da Gama reached Kappad near Kozhikode in Kerala in May 1498. Aim to monopolise spice trade and spread Christianity.
2. Arrival and Aim - British: Came as traders in early 17th century as British East India Company under royal charter of Queen Elizabeth I. Aim to buy cotton textiles, silk, indigo, spices and sell in Europe for profit, later turned from traders to rulers.
3. Method of Control - Portuguese: Captured strategic ports, most importantly Goa in 1510 as capital and naval base; extended trading posts on Malabar and Coromandel coasts; introduced cartaz system - compulsory pass for ships in Arabian Sea, ships without permits attacked/seized; established Goa Inquisition in 1560, persecuted Hindus, Muslims, Jews, destroyed temples, forced conversions.
4. Method of Control - British: Set up factories at Surat, Madras, Bombay, Calcutta, built forts around trading posts; used military-trick and alliances - Battle of Plassey 1757 defeated Siraj-ud-Daulah with betrayal of Mir Jafar, control over Bengal; Battle of Buxar 1764 got Diwani rights of Bengal, Bihar, Orissa; used Divide and Rule, Subsidiary Alliance introduced by Lord Wellesley 1798 - Hyderabad first, and Doctrine of Lapse by Lord Dalhousie - annexed Jhansi, Satara, Nagpur by refusing adopted heirs.
5. Economic Impact - Portuguese: Monopolised spice trade between India and Europe for nearly a century, immense naval profits.
6. Economic Impact - British: Imposed high cash taxes, forced cash crops like indigo, cotton instead of food grains, caused Bengal Famine 1770 and Great Famine 1876-78; Drain of Wealth explained by Dadabhai Naoroji and R.C. Dutt; destroyed Indian textile, iron, steel, paper industries by unfair duties.

Final:
Portuguese colonisation was mainly naval-trade monopoly through ports, cartaz passes and religious persecution centred at Goa, declined by 17th century with Dutch, British, French rivals; British colonisation was slow trader-to-ruler takeover through battles, revenue rights, Divide and Rule, Subsidiary Alliance and Doctrine of Lapse, leading to two centuries of economic exploitation, famines, industrial decline and administrative control.

Q4. Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha elections

Steps:
1. House: Lok Sabha - House of the People, Lower House; Rajya Sabha - Council of States, Upper House, Permanent House.
2. Mode of Election: Lok Sabha - directly elected by citizens above 18 years; Rajya Sabha - indirectly elected by elected MLAs of State Assemblies, not directly by citizens.
3. System: Lok Sabha - First-Past-The-Post (FPTP) system where candidate with most votes wins even without 50%; country divided into 543 constituencies. Rajya Sabha - Single Transferable Vote (STV) system with proportional representation ensuring smaller parties and states fair chance.
4. Composition and Term: Lok Sabha - elected MPs, every five years, forms Union government, leader of majority becomes Prime Minister. Rajya Sabha - 245 members, 233 elected by MLAs and 12 nominated by President for literature, science, art, social service; never dissolved, one-third retire every two years, each member six-year term.
5. Role: Lok Sabha - direct role of citizens in state and national governance, backbone of parliamentary democracy. Rajya Sabha - voice of states, safeguards interests of smaller regions, reviews, debates and passes laws as check on rushed decisions.

Final:
| Lok Sabha elections | Rajya Sabha elections |
| :--- | :--- |
| Direct, FPTP, 543 constituencies, 5 years, elect MPs | Indirect by MLAs, STV proportional, 245 members (233 elected + 12 nominated), permanent, 6-year term with 1/3 retirement every 2 years |

Q5. Labour-intensive production and Capital-intensive production

Steps:
1. Meaning - Labour-intensive: Industries where human effort and skills play most important role in making goods and services, depend less on machines.
2. Meaning - Capital-intensive: Industries where machines, technology and large financial investments play dominant role in creating goods and services.
3. Examples - Labour-intensive: Agriculture, handicrafts, cottage industries, small shops, bangle making where people work directly with natural resources or simple tools.
4. Examples - Capital-intensive: Automobile factories, semiconductor plants, space research centres where machines and technology carry out most work.
5. Features - Labour-intensive: Human Effort as primary resource; Worker Skills decide productivity and quality; Time and Energy - manual work slower, needs greater physical-mental energy; Limited Technology - simple tools, traditional techniques, low cost but limits large-scale production.
6. Features - Capital-intensive: Machines and Technology allow larger quantities, higher accuracy and speed; Large Investment to set up factories, buy and maintain equipment; High Efficiency, mechanisation makes production faster, cheaper per unit; Dependence on Experts - skilled engineers, scientists, technicians to design, monitor and repair.
7. Role of other factor: Labour-intensive still uses simple capital; Capital-intensive still needs skilled human capital to supervise machines, proving human effort and technology must complement.

Final:
| Labour-Intensive Production | Capital-Intensive Production |
| :--- | :--- |
| Depends mainly on physical strength, patience, creativity of workers | Depends heavily on modern machines, advanced tools, money |
| Slower, more time-energy, traditional tools | Faster, bulk supply, automated, high investment and efficiency |
| Example: farming with bullocks-manual labour, handicrafts, bangle making | Example: textile factory with automated looms, automobile factory, modern farming with tractors, harvesters, drip irrigation |

II. A. Case Study Based Questions:

During a visit to a science exhibition, Rohan learned how coal, petroleum, and natural gas power industries and transport systems. His guide explained that these are non-renewable resources, formed over millions of years from the remains of plants and animals. Since they cannot be replaced quickly, their overuse leads to shortages and environmental problems like pollution and global warming. Rohan understood that switching to renewable resources such as solar and wind energy is essential for a sustainable future. Questions:

B. Case Study Based Questions:

During his visit to a museum, Rohan learned about the Battle of Plassey (1757), which marked the beginning of British political control in India. The East India Company, led by Robert Clive, defeated Siraj-ud-Daulah with the secret support of Mir Jafar, gaining control over Bengal—the richest Indian province. This victory allowed the British to collect revenue, control trade, and slowly expand their rule. What began as trade soon turned into colonial domination, changing India's economy and governance for over two centuries. Questions:

Q5. The first major peasant movement against the British indigo planters occurred in: a. Punjab b. Bengal c. Bihar d. Madras

✅ Answer: b. Bengal
Reason: Indigo planters forced farmers to grow the blue dye crop and the fermentation process poisoned the soil, causing a decade-long decline in rice yields across Bengal.

Q6. The Great Rebellion of 1857 resulted in: a. End of Mughal rule and beginning of British Crown rule b. Complete independence of India c. Return of Indian rulers to power d. Defeat of European powers in India

✅ Answer: a. End of Mughal rule and beginning of British Crown rule
Reason: In 1858, the British Crown took direct control of India from the East India Company, marking the beginning of the British Raj.

Q7. What does the Model Code of Conduct (MCC) prevent during elections? a. Peaceful campaigning b. Use of money, power and government machinery c. Free media d. Opinion polls

✅ Answer: b. Use of money, power and government machinery
Reason: The main aim of the MCC is to stop the misuse of power, money, or government machinery that could give unfair advantage.

Q8. Who among the following is elected through an indirect election? a. Prime Minister b. Chief Minister c. President d. Member of Lok Sabha

✅ Answer: c. President
Reason: The President is chosen by an Electoral College of elected MPs and MLAs and listed under Features as Indirect Election.

Q3. Assertion (A): The Maratha navy was created to safeguard trade and defend the western coast from foreign powers. Reason (R): The Marathas depended only on the naval fleet for coastal security.

⚠️ Answer: Not covered in this chapter — NEVER guess or skip it.

Q4. Assertion (A): The Portuguese were the first Europeans to arrive in India by sea. Reason (R): Vasco da Gama reached Calicut in 1498, opening the sea route between Europe and Asia.

✅ Answer: a. Both Assertion (A) and Reason (R) are true and Reason (R) is the correct explanation of Assertion (A).
Reason: The Portuguese were the first Europeans to arrive by sea when Vasco da Gama reached Kappad near Kozhikode in May 1498 after sailing around Africa.

Q5. Assertion (A): The Dutch were defeated by Travancore in the Battle of Colachel. Reason (R): After this defeat, the Dutch expanded their rule to cover the whole of southern India.

✅ Answer: c. Assertion (A) is true but Reason (R) is false.
Reason: After Colachel in 1741 under King Marthanda Varma, the Dutch shifted focus to Southeast Asia and their influence in India gradually declined.

Q6. Assertion (A): The Election Commission of India works under the control of the central government. Reason (R): This ensures that elections are conducted smoothly across the country.

✅ Answer: d. Assertion (A) is false but Reason (R) is true.
Reason: The ECI was created in 1950 as an independent constitutional body under Article 324, not under direct control of government, to hold free and fair elections.

Q7. Assertion (A): Low voter turnout and influence of money are signs of a healthy democracy. Reason (R): These problems show that citizens are aware of their rights and actively participate in elections.

✅ Answer: d. Assertion (A) is false but Reason (R) is true.
Reason: Low voter turnout, influence of money power and criminalisation are listed as challenges that reduce fairness and weaken trust in democracy.

Q8. Assertion (A): Capital is a natural factor of production that occurs freely in nature. Reason (R): Machines, tools and equipment are examples of capital used in production.

✅ Answer: d. Assertion (A) is false but Reason (R) is true.
Reason: Capital includes all man-made resources such as money, machines, tools, equipment and buildings, unlike land which includes natural resources.

Q9. Assertion (A): Entrepreneurs play a key role in combining different factors of production and taking risks to earn profits. Reason (R): Without entrepreneurs, factors like land, labour and capital cannot function effectively together.

✅ Answer: a. Both Assertion (A) and Reason (R) are true and Reason (R) is the correct explanation of Assertion (A).
Reason: Entrepreneurs bring together land, labour, capital, technology and human capital in right proportion, for on their own these resources cannot be used effectively.

✅ Answer:
Step 1: In 1757 at Palashi the British under Robert Clive defeated Nawab Siraj-ud-Daulah despite being smaller in number.
Step 2: Victory gave control over Bengal, the richest province, and made the Company kingmaker deciding rulers.
Final: Beginning of British political dominance from traders to rulers

Q2. How did the East India Company benefit from control over Bengal?

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Gained Diwani revenue rights of Bengal, Bihar and Orissa with fertile land and trade resources as base for expansion.
Step 2: Huge revenue called Plassey Plunder financed expansion and helped finance Industrial Revolution in Britain.
Final: Collected revenue, controlled trade and expanded rule for nearly two centuries

Q3. What role did Mir Jafar play in helping the British win the battle?

✅ Answer:
Step 1: Mir Jafar, commander of Nawab's army, made secret agreement with Clive to betray Siraj-ud-Daulah for promise of next Nawab.
Step 2: His forces forming most of Nawab's army did not fight, allowing British to win easily despite being outnumbered.
Final: Betrayal caused British victory, Mir Jafar remembered as synonym for traitor

Q9. Which of the following statements about production is correct? a. Only land is sufficient for production b. Labour and capital can work independently c. Technology is not a part of production d. All factors of production must work together

✅ Answer: d. All factors of production must work together
Reason: Production becomes successful only when all factors — land, labour, capital, entrepreneurship and technology — work together in harmony.

Q10. In capital-intensive industries, production mainly depends on: a. Advanced machines and technology b. Manual work c. Hand tools only d. Natural resources alone

✅ Answer: a. Advanced machines and technology
Reason: Capital-intensive production is where machines, technology and large financial investments play the dominant role, e.g., automobile factories.
