7Es EXPLAIN AND ELABORATE - 1

Imagine setting sail on a long voyage into the unknown—crossing oceans, facing storms and discovering distant lands filled with treasures and new cultures. This was the age when powerful European nations began exploring the world in search of wealth, fame and dominance. What started as a race for trade soon turned into a fierce struggle for control. With advanced ships, weapons and strategies, countries like Portugal, Spain, Britain, France and the Netherlands began conquering new territories across Asia, Africa and the Americas. They called this expansion colonialism—a system where one nation ruled over another for its own benefit. India, known for its riches in spices, silk, cotton and precious metals, quickly attracted these European powers. One after another, they arrived on their shores as traders but gradually turned into rulers. Ports became battlefields, trade turned into control and the

quest for profit led to centuries of foreign domination. Colonialism changed India's economy, politics and culture forever—transforming a land once known for prosperity into one exploited for its wealth. In this chapter, we will explore how colonialism spread across the world, how it reached India and the lasting impact it left on our nation's history.

THE AGE OF COLONIALISM

Colonialism or the practice of one country controlling and exploiting another, was not something new in world history. From ancient times, powerful empires tried to expand their territories by conquering new lands. The basic idea of colonialism is that one country takes control of another region, establishes settlements there and then imposes its own political, economic and cultural systems on the people. In simple words, the colonisers not only ruled over the land but also tried to change the way people lived, worked and thought.

This was not a recent development. In fact, colonialism can be traced back to the time of the great empires of the 1st millennium BCE, such as the Persian and Roman empires, which expanded by conquering neighbouring territories. Later, in the 1st millennium CE, the spread of religions like Christianity and Islam also brought colonisation, as regions that adopted these new faiths often came under the political and cultural influence of foreign rulers.

However, from the 15th century onwards, colonialism entered a completely new stage. This was when European powers such as Spain, Portugal, Britain, France and the Netherlands began expanding rapidly across the world. With the help of advanced ships, improved navigation skills and powerful weapons, they were able to travel far beyond Europe. These countries built colonies in distant places such as Asia, Africa, the Americas, Australia and the Pacific islands.

This period is often called the Age of Exploration or the Age of Colonialism, because European nations were competing fiercely with each other to capture new territories. They wanted to prove their power and wealth by expanding their empires. Many of these conquests were achieved through military campaigns that were extremely violent. Native populations often suffered greatly—thousands were killed in massacres, while many others were forced into slavery to work for the benefit of the colonisers.

EXTENDED LEARNING

The word colony comes from the Latin term colonus, meaning 'farmer' or 'settler.' Ancient empires like the Phoenicians and Romans set up colonies around the Mediterranean Sea for trade. However, modern European colonialism went far beyond trade—it aimed at total political, cultural and economic control of the colonies.

[IMAGE_START] A small landscape illustration featuring a tree on the right side with a rocky cliff face, and a horizon line showing distant hills or mountains in the background. [IMAGE_END]

Causes of Colonialism

Colonialism did not happen for a single reason but was driven by many factors that pushed European nations to expand their control across the world. Countries competed with each other for power, wealth and influence while also trying to spread their religion and increase their knowledge.

Following are some of the causes that explain why European powers established colonies in Asia, Africa and the Americas:

Political Competition

Colonialism was strongly driven by political rivalry among European nations. Each country in Europe wanted to prove that it was more powerful and influential than its rivals. Capturing colonies across the world became a way to display strength and build prestige. The more colonies a nation controlled, the more respected and feared it became in global politics. For example, Spain and Portugal divided South America between themselves through a treaty, while Britain and France were constantly competing for control over India and Africa. Colonies were seen as a sign of greatness and countries believed that ruling over large territories would make them world powers. This race for dominance often led to wars, naval battles and long rivalries between European powers. Thus, political competition was a major force pushing these nations to expand aggressively.

DID YOU KNOW?

To maintain power overseas, European empires built some of the world's first global naval networks. By the 18th century, Britain had over 600 warships, while Spain and Portugal controlled sea routes connecting Asia, Africa and the Americas—making the oceans the real battlegrounds of empire.

[IMAGE_START] An illustration of a fort or castle wall with towers. In the foreground, there are camels and soldiers on horseback, suggesting a historical battle or patrol scene. [IMAGE_END]

Economic Advantages

Another major reason for colonialism was the desire for wealth and economic gain. Colonies offered abundant supply of resources that were not available in Europe. Regions in Asia, Africa and the Americas were rich in raw materials such as spices, cotton, silk, sugar, coffee, gold and silver. At first, European countries set up trading posts to buy and sell these products. But soon, they began taking complete control of these regions to secure steady supplies.

Colonies were also forced to buy manufactured goods produced in European factories, which meant the colonisers made huge profits both ways. In addition, the colonisers often looted or plundered valuable resources and treasures directly. These colonies gave European nations access to new markets, fresh trade routes and natural resources, which allowed them to grow richer. The wealth collected from the colonies later helped to fund industrial revolutions in Europe, making these countries even stronger.

DID YOU KNOW?
Christian missionaries often learned local languages to translate the Bible and spread religion. The first printing press in India was set up by the Portuguese in 1556 at Goa, where they printed religious texts in Konkani and Tamil, making it one of Asia's earliest printing centres.

[IMAGE_START] Illustration of a building with arched doorways and windows. Several figures are walking in front of the building. The building appears to be a historical structure, possibly a printing press or a church, with a tower-like structure on the right side. [IMAGE_END]

EXTENDED LEARNING
Silver mined in the Americas became the backbone of global trade during the colonial period. Much of it was used to buy Asian goods like silk and spices. This silver eventually flowed into India and China, connecting the economies of three continents for the first time in history.

[IMAGE_START] Illustration of a landscape with a large tree on the left side. The tree has a thick trunk and sparse leaves. In the background, there are rolling hills and smaller trees. The ground is brown and textured. [IMAGE_END]

Religious Motives

Religion too played an important role in the spread of colonialism. Many European rulers and Christian missionaries felt it was their duty to spread Christianity in the newly conquered lands. They built churches, converted local people and tried to change traditional cultural practices. This was often presented as a 'civilising mission,' where Europeans claimed they were bringing progress and modernity to 'backward' societies.

However, in reality, spreading religion was also a way to establish greater control. Once people gave up their traditional beliefs, they became easier to influence and rule. Colonisers often described local populations as 'savage,' 'primitive,' or 'barbaric' to justify their dominance. By weakening the existing culture and replacing it with foreign values, colonisers were able to strengthen their political power and expand their authority.

Scientific Inquiry and Exploration

The Age of Colonialism was also closely linked with the idea of exploration and scientific curiosity. Many European sailors and explorers were motivated by the desire to discover new lands, unknown sea routes and different peoples. Famous explorers such as Vasco da Gama, who found a sea route to India, Christopher Columbus, who reached the Americas and Ferdinand Magellan, whose expedition was the first to sail around the world, played a huge role in changing how people understood the Earth.

[IMAGE_START] Map showing the route of Vasco da Gama's first voyage to India. The map displays the Atlantic Ocean, Africa, the Arabian Sea, and the Indian Ocean. A red line traces the route from Lisbon, Portugal, down the west coast of Africa, around the Cape of Good Hope, up the east coast of Africa (passing Mozambique, Sofala, Malindi, Kilwa, Mogadishu), across the Arabian Sea to Aden, and finally to Goa, Cannanore, and Calicut in India. Labels include: Portugal, Lisbon, Canary Is., Cape Verde Is., St. Helena, Cape of Good Hope, Mozambique, Sofala, Malindi, Kilwa, Mogadishu, Aden, Hormuz, Goa, Cannanore, Calicut, India, Arabian Sea, Indian Ocean, Atlantic Ocean. A scale bar indicates distances in miles and kilometers. A note says "MAP NOT TO SCALE FOR EDUCATIONAL PURPOSE ONLY". [IMAGE_END]

Vasco da Gama's first voyage to India

These long voyages were not just about trade or conquest — they were also about gaining knowledge. Europeans wanted to study the geography of the planet, understand its climate and learn about the natural history of new regions, including plants, animals and minerals. Scientific curiosity and the desire to map the world encouraged them to travel across dangerous oceans and explore unknown territories.

As a result of these discoveries, continents became more connected than ever before. Goods, ideas and

even cultures started moving between Europe, Asia, Africa and the Americas. However, this also gave Europeans the advantage of controlling global trade and politics, since they now had the maps, ships and knowledge needed to dominate. In other words, scientific exploration not only expanded human knowledge but also helped Europeans tighten their colonial grip on the world.

Impact of Colonialism

Colonialism changed the history of many nations, including India, in very deep and long-lasting ways. It brought foreign control, economic exploitation, cultural disruption and widespread poverty, while only offering limited benefits that mainly served the colonisers.

Following are some of the major impacts of colonialism that explain how it affected the lives of people and shaped the future of societies:

  • Loss of Independence and Freedom: Colonialism meant that local rulers and traditional governments were replaced by foreign powers who took control. The people of the colonies no longer had the right to make their own decisions about politics, economy or society. Instead, everything was decided by the colonisers, often in ways that only benefited them. This complete loss of independence and freedom made people feel powerless in their own land.
  • Exploitation of Resources: Colonisers took away valuable raw materials such as cotton, spices, timber, minerals and precious metals from the colonies. These natural resources were shipped to Europe, where they were used in factories to make goods. The finished products were then sold back at high prices, giving profits only to the colonisers. The local people rarely got any benefit from their own wealth and were often left poorer.
  • Disruption of Traditional Life: The arrival of colonial powers changed the everyday lives of ordinary people in harmful ways. Farmers were forced to grow cash crops like indigo, sugarcane and cotton instead of food crops that their families needed. This caused food shortages and even terrible famines, where millions died. Artisans and craftsmen also lost their livelihoods because cheap, factory-made goods from Europe flooded local markets, destroying traditional industries and skills that had supported families for generations.
  • Imposition of Foreign Values and Culture: Colonisers imposed their own values, systems and culture on the people they ruled. Schools, laws and administration were redesigned to serve European interests, not local needs. Local customs, languages and traditions were often insulted and dismissed as 'backward,' while European culture was shown as more 'advanced.' This created a sense of shame and cultural inferiority among the colonised people, weakening their confidence in their own identity.
  • Limited Benefits of Technology and Infrastructure: It is true that colonial powers introduced some modern systems such as railways, telegraphs, postal services and Western-style schools. These changes helped connect regions and improved travel and communication. But these developments were mainly created for the benefit of the colonisers, who used them to move soldiers quickly, transport raw materials to ports and strengthen their control. Ordinary people rarely got the full benefit of these technologies.
  • Poverty, Hardship and Exploitation: For the colonised populations, colonialism mostly brought suffering and hardship. Local industries collapsed, taxes were increased and many people lost their land and jobs. Poverty became common and social inequalities grew worse. While the colonisers became richer and more powerful by using the colonies' wealth, the colonised communities were left weaker, exploited and burdened with hunger and misery.

End of Colonialism

Colonialism lasted for several centuries, with European powers ruling over vast parts of the world. It reached its peak between the 18th and 19th centuries, when almost the entire continents of Asia, Africa and Latin America were controlled by countries like Britain, France, Spain and Portugal. During this time, the European nations grew very powerful, while the colonised regions lost much of their freedom and resources.

By the mid-20th century, however, resistance against colonial powers became stronger than ever. People in colonies started demanding freedom and equality and powerful nationalist movements began to grow. Leaders in countries like India, Indonesia, Ghana and many others inspired people to unite against foreign rule. The suffering caused by colonial exploitation and the desire for self-respect gave great strength to these movements.

World War II (1939–1945) also weakened European colonial powers. Countries such as Britain and France had spent huge amounts of money and energy during the war and could no longer control their vast empires effectively. At the same time, new global powers like the United States and the Soviet Union supported decolonisation and the idea of freedom for all nations. As a result, many colonies gained independence in a process called decolonisation. Nations in Asia, Africa, the Middle East and the Caribbean gradually achieved self-rule. For example, India became independent in 1947, followed by many African countries in the 1950s and 1960s. Although political independence was finally achieved, the impact of colonialism did not disappear overnight. Colonised countries were left with weak economies, deep social divisions and political systems shaped by their former rulers. Even today, many developing nations continue to face problems that are rooted in the colonial past. Thus, while the age of colonialism ended in the 20th century, its long-lasting effects are still visible in the modern world.

The structure has arched windows and intricate detailing, resembling traditional Indian architecture. Inside the box is a list of 5 sentences to correct. [IMAGE_END]

Correct and rewrite the following sentences.
1. Colonialism was a modern idea that began only in the 18th century.
2. European nations expanded peacefully across the world, without any wars or rivalries.
3. The colonisers respected local cultures and traditions and encouraged them to grow.
4. Religion had no role in spreading colonial control over new lands.
5. The Age of Colonialism completely ignored science and exploration.

7Es EXPLAIN AND ELABORATE - 2
EUROPEANS IN INDIA
Before the rise of colonialism, India was considered one of the richest and most advanced economies in the entire world. Historians estimate that during the medieval period, India contributed nearly one-fourth of the world's GDP. This enormous wealth came from a strong and productive agricultural system, thriving inland and overseas trade and skilled artisans who produced high-quality goods admired everywhere. Indian cities and ports were bustling centers of trade, connecting Asia, Africa and Europe. Indian goods were famous across continents. The textiles of Bengal and Gujarat, such as fine muslin (a delicate cloth) and colourful cotton fabrics, were in great demand in European markets. These textiles were so soft, light and beautifully designed that they were considered luxury items. Similarly, spices from the Malabar and Coromandel coasts—including pepper, cardamom and cloves—were valued almost like gold in Europe because they were used to preserve food, add rich flavours and even in medicines. Apart from textiles and spices, India was also well known for its iron and steel products, which were of superior quality and for a wide variety of handicrafts such as jewellery, carpets and pottery, which showed the artistic skills and fine craftsmanship of Indian artisans. This immense prosperity naturally attracted traders, explorers and adventurers from Europe. Starting in the late 15th century, Europeans such as the Portuguese, Dutch, British and French began sailing to India, hoping to profit from this trade. At first, they came only as traders, setting up small factories and trading posts along the coast, where they exchanged European goods for Indian spices, textiles and other products. Over time, however, the competition between these European nations grew stronger. In order to gain advantage over each other, they began to interfere in Indian politics, forming alliances with local rulers and gradually using their superior military strength and advanced ships to capture territories. What started as a simple search for trade opportunities slowly turned into political and territorial control. This marked the beginning of European colonialism in India, a long period during which India's economy, administration and society would be deeply transformed—some changes were modernising, but most caused exploitation and hardship for Indians.

PORTUGUESE IN INDIA
The Portuguese were the first Europeans to arrive in India by sea. In May 1498, the Portuguese explorer Vasco da Gama reached Kappad (near Kozhikode in Kerala) after sailing around Africa's Cape of Good

[IMAGE_START] A small green leaf illustration at the bottom center of the page. [IMAGE_END]

Hope. This historic journey opened the way for direct trade between Europe and India, bypassing older land routes controlled by Arab and Turkish traders and marked the beginning of European colonisation in India. Though he was welcomed at first, Vasco da Gama's aggressive methods soon damaged relations. On his second voyage in 1502, he seized, tortured and killed Indian merchants and even bombarded Calicut from the sea, creating hostility among local rulers.

To expand their power, the Portuguese captured strategic ports, most importantly Goa in 1510, which became the capital of their colony. From Goa, they built a strong naval base and extended control over several trading posts along the Malabar and Coromandel coasts. To tighten their monopoly, they introduced the cartaz system—a compulsory pass for Indian and foreign ships sailing in the Arabian Sea. Ships without permits were attacked or seized and their goods were confiscated. This allowed the Portuguese to monopolise the spice trade between India and Europe for nearly a century, bringing them immense profits and naval power. Alongside commercial exploitation, they also tried to spread Christianity. In 1560, the Goa Inquisition was established, which persecuted Hindus, Muslims, Jews and even Christian converts suspected of practising old beliefs. Many temples were destroyed, forced conversions took place and harsh punishments created fear and resentment. The Inquisition was finally abolished in 1812, but it left behind a bitter legacy.

Despite their dominance, the Portuguese did not go unchallenged. Indian rulers and local communities resisted their control. One of the most famous examples was Rani Abbakka Chowta of Ullal (near Mangaluru, Karnataka), who bravely fought against repeated Portuguese naval attacks

[IMAGE_START] Portrait of Vasco da Gama. The image shows a man with a long white beard, wearing a dark hat and dark clothing with a cross pendant. He is holding an open book or scroll in his hands. The caption below reads "Vasco da Gama". [IMAGE_END]

in the 16th century. She became a legendary figure and the symbol of courage and defiance against foreign power.

Although the Portuguese were the first Europeans to firmly establish themselves in India, their influence gradually declined with the arrival of stronger rivals such as the Dutch, British and French. By the 17th century, their monopoly was broken, but their cultural legacy has survived in India.

Even today, in places like Goa, Daman and Diu, Portuguese influence can still be seen in architecture, churches, festivals and language, reminding us of the first phase of European presence in India.

EXTENDED LEARNING

Goa became a blend of Indian and Portuguese cultures. Even today, the architectural styles of Old Goa—like the Basilica of Bom Jesus and Se Cathedral—are recognised as UNESCO World Heritage Sites. The Goan carnival, music and food reflect this 500-year-old fusion of East and West.

[IMAGE_START] A postage stamp featuring Rani Abbakka Chowta. The stamp shows a woman in traditional white and gold attire holding a sword in her right hand. The text on the stamp includes "500", "INDIA", and Kannada script. The caption below the image reads "Rani Abbakka Chowta, queen of Ullal". [IMAGE_END]

THE DUTCH IN INDIA

The Dutch, from the Netherlands, arrived in India in the early 17th century. Unlike the Portuguese, they were not focused on conquering territories. Their main aim was commercial dominance, especially in the profitable spice trade. In 1602, they set up the Dutch East India Company (VOC), which soon became one of the wealthiest trading companies in the world.

They established trading posts across India: on the west coast in Surat, Bharuch, Cochin (Kochi) and parts of Malabar; and on the east coast in Pulicat (near Chennai), Nagapattinam (Tamil Nadu) and Masulipatnam (present-day Machilipatnam andhra Pradesh). Their strongest base was in Malabar, Kerala, where they even displaced the Portuguese from several centres. They traded in pepper, cardamom, cloves and textiles, exporting them to Europe in exchange for silver, copper and other goods, which made their ships and company extremely profitable.

However, the Dutch soon faced tough competition from both other European powers like the British and French and from Indian rulers. Their biggest defeat came in 1741 at the Battle of Colachel, where the forces of Travancore under King Marthanda Varma decisively defeated the Dutch both on land

and at sea. The Travancore kingdom, located in the southern part of present-day Kerala, became one of the earliest examples in Asia where an Indian power successfully repelled a European colonial force.

[IMAGE_START] A painting depicting a historical scene. In the foreground, a man in European clothing (Dutch) is bowing or kneeling before a seated Indian ruler (King Marthanda Varma) who is wearing traditional attire and a turban. To the left, another figure sits on a throne-like chair under an umbrella. In the background, there is a coastal scene with ships and other figures standing. A flag is visible on the right. [IMAGE_END]

A depiction of the surrender of Dutch forces to King Marthanda Varma of Travancore after the Battle of Colachel, 1741 CE (Padmanabhapuram Palace).

After Colachel, the Dutch shifted their focus to Southeast Asia, especially Indonesia, where they had stronger control. Their influence in India gradually declined and they never became as powerful here as the Portuguese, British or French.

Even though their presence was limited, the Dutch left behind forts, coins and trade records, which show the global connections of India during this period and the intense competition between European powers for Indian trade.

[IMAGE_START] Two close-up images of old coins. The top coin features a coat of arms or crest. The bottom coin features a monogram with the letters "VOC" intertwined. [IMAGE_END]

Dutch East India Company (VOC) Coins

EXTENDED LEARNING

The Dutch East India Company, founded in 1602, was the world's first multinational corporation. It issued shares, maintained a private army and even made treaties—behaving like a state within a state. This company model later inspired the creation of the British and French East India Companies.

[IMAGE_START] An illustration of a tree growing on a rocky outcrop with a landscape and hills in the background. [IMAGE_END]

THE FRENCH IN INDIA

The French entered India later than the Portuguese and Dutch. Their first trading post was set up at Surat in 1668, followed by the establishment of their main base at Pondicherry (present-day Puducherry) in 1764, where they organised their East India Company, known as the Compagnie des Indes Orientales. They also created smaller settlements at Chandernagore (Bengal), Mahé (Kerala) and Karaikal (Tamil Nadu), which became important centres of French presence. From these bases, they developed ambitious plans to build an empire in India.

[IMAGE_START] An old map titled "PLAN DE PONDICHERY EN 1741". It shows a fortified city layout with a grid of streets. There is a legend or key at the top with letters A through P and corresponding French labels. The bottom of the map shows a large fortification facing the sea. [IMAGE_END]

A 1764 plan of Pondicherry showing the city enclosed in fortifications and grid planning. The large structure facing the sea (at the bottom) was the fort. (The top of the plan points to the west.)

One of the most significant French leaders was Joseph François Dupleix, Governor-General of French India from 1742 to 1754. Dupleix realised that if the French wanted to compete with the British, they needed more than trade—they required military strength and political influence. He pioneered strategies later used by the British, such as training Indian sepoys in European military techniques and interfering in local succession disputes to install puppet rulers dependent on French support. This gave the French

lead to the rivalry of French & British (Handwritten note)

indirect control over Indian politics and strengthened their influence in southern India.

However, these policies led to direct conflict with the British East India Company, resulting in the Carnatic Wars (1746–1763). The French achieved some early victories under Dupleix, including the capture of Madras (present-day Chennai) in 1746, but the British eventually gained the upper hand due to their stronger navy, better organisation and larger resources. The decisive defeats in these wars forced the French to abandon their larger ambitions, leaving them with only Pondicherry and a few smaller enclaves. Unlike the Portuguese, the French generally avoided interfering in social and religious matters, though a rare exception was in 1748, when the Vedapurishwaran temple at Pondicherry was destroyed at the request of Jesuit priests and with Dupleix's support.

[IMAGE_START] A black and white portrait of a man with powdered hair, wearing a coat and cravat. Caption: Joseph François Dupleix. [IMAGE_END]

DID YOU KNOW?

The French influence in India left behind many enduring traces. The French language continued to be spoken in Pondicherry schools until 1954 and the French-style street grid—wide, tree-lined boulevards meeting at right angles—still defines the old town's layout today.

ENTER THE BRITISH

The British East India Company first came to India as traders in the early 17th century. Their main aim was to buy valuable Indian goods such as cotton textiles, silk, indigo and spices and then sell them in Europe for huge profits. Indian products were famous worldwide for their fine quality and European markets were eager for them.

The Company was established as a trading organisation under a royal charter granted by Queen Elizabeth I, which even gave it powers such as maintaining its own private army. At first, the British presented themselves as mere traders, which made it easier for them to gain acceptance from Indian rulers. In the 17th century, they set up trading centres called factories in places like Surat, Madras, Bombay and Calcutta. Since India had a long tradition of foreign trade, most local rulers did not mind the British presence and welcomed the extra commerce.

[IMAGE_START] A color portrait of a woman in an elaborate dress with a large white ruff collar. Caption: Queen Elizabeth I. [IMAGE_END]

Gradually, the Company began building forts around their trading posts. These forts not only protected their warehouses and ships but also gave them military strength. What looked like simple trading arrangements were, in reality, the first steps in a long-term plan. Over time, Britain went from being a foreign merchant power to dominating the Indian subcontinent for nearly two centuries, changing the course of Indian history.

[IMAGE_START] A historical painting depicting a battle scene outside a fort. Soldiers are gathered, some on horseback, cannons are visible. Caption: Carnatic Wars, capture of Madras. [IMAGE_END]

By the end of the Carnatic Wars, the French dream of establishing a large Indian empire had ended. Unlike the British, they could not expand across the subcontinent and had to remain confined to a few trading posts. Still, their influence survived in architecture, culture and trade. Even today, Pondicherry reflects French heritage, with its distinctive buildings, streets and traditions, reminding us of the period when France tried to compete with other European powers in India.

96

From Traders to Rulers

The British conquest of India is one of history's most notable examples of how a trading company could transform into an imperial power. Unlike the classic invasions of the past, the British takeover was slow, calculated and often disguised as commerce rather than outright conquest.

A major turning point came in 1757 at the Battle of Plassey, where the British defeated the Nawab of Bengal, Siraj-ud-Daulah, with the secret support of traitors like Mir Jafar. This gave them control over Bengal, the richest province in India, whose wealth and resources became the base for British expansion.

Another key victory was the Battle of Buxar (1764), where they defeated the combined armies of the Nawab of Bengal, the Nawab of Awadh and the Mughal Emperor. After this, the Company received the Diwani Bihars (revenue collection rights) of Bengal, Bihar and Orissa, making them rulers in practice, not just traders.

By the late 18th and early 19th centuries, the East India Company was no longer simply buying and selling goods. It was collecting revenue, running administration and controlling vast territories. Its agents became power brokers in Indian politics, deciding the fate of rulers and shaping kingdoms to suit their interests. Thus, the Company's modest trading beginnings concealed its imperial ambitions, which gradually turned into full-scale colonial rule.

[IMAGE_START] Illustration inside a box titled "LET'S EXPLORE". It features a cartoon man in a brown suit standing on top of a tall, reddish-brown pedestal or column. He is pointing upwards with his right index finger. At the base of the column, there are silhouettes of a crowd of people looking up. [IMAGE_END]

The Strategy of 'Divide and Rule'

The British knew they could not conquer India through force alone, so they used clever strategies to expand their influence. Their most famous method was the policy of 'divide and rule.' On the surface, they maintained the appearance of traders, but behind the scenes, they cultivated political relationships with local rulers, offering military support to some against their rivals.

Whenever succession disputes or rivalries arose between Indian rulers, the British inserted themselves into these conflicts and emerged as power brokers rather than as direct invaders. They supported one side and in return, they gained land, money or loyalty. This helped them steadily increase their influence without appearing too threatening.

The British were equally skilled at exploiting divisions within Indian society itself. They identified and often encouraged tensions between regional kingdoms, ruling houses and even religious communities, which made it harder for Indians to unite against them. By weakening unity and keeping rulers divided, the British ensured their dominance and laid the foundations of nearly two centuries of colonial rule.

British Expansion and Strategies

The British did not conquer India overnight; instead, they used a series of clever strategies, battles and policies to expand their control step by step. They combined military strength with political tricks, alliances and unfair laws to weaken Indian rulers and increase their own power.

Following are some of the important strategies through which the British turned from traders into rulers and established their dominance in India:

Battle of Plassey (1757)

The Battle of Plassey was a turning point in Indian history and shows how the British used tricks and alliances to expand their power. By the mid-18th century, tensions had grown between Siraj-ud-Daulah, the Nawab of Bengal and the East India Company officials led by Robert Clive. The Company wanted more control over Bengal's trade and resources, while the Nawab wanted to limit their growing influence.

Although the British army was smaller in number, Clive cleverly looked for discontented people inside the Nawab's court. He made a secret agreement with Mir Jafar, one of the Nawab's commanders, promising to make him the next Nawab in exchange for betraying Siraj-ud-Daulah. The battle took place at Palashi (spelt Plassey by the British), about 150 kilometres north of present-day Kolkata.

Some French soldiers came to support the Nawab, but Mir Jafar’s forces—who made up most of the Nawab’s army—did not fight. Their betrayal allowed the British to win easily despite being outnumbered.

[IMAGE_START] A historical painting depicting a meeting. In the foreground, a group of men in 18th-century military uniforms (red coats, white breeches) are gathered. One man is on horseback on the left. In the background, a large elephant with a howdah (seat) on its back is visible, carrying several people. Flags are being held. Caption: Robert Clive's meeting with Mir Jafar, Battle of Plassey [IMAGE_END]

This victory gave the Company control over Bengal, the richest province in India. Bengal’s huge revenue, fertile land and trade resources became the foundation of British power in India. The Company also emerged as a kingmaker, deciding which Indian rulers would stay in power. Even today, the name ‘Mir Jafar’ is used in India as a synonym for traitor, showing how strongly this betrayal is remembered. The Battle of Plassey marked the beginning of British political dominance in India, turning them from traders into rulers.

EXTENDED LEARNING
The fortune the British gained from Bengal after the Battle of Plassey was so massive that it was called ‘Plassey Plunder’ in England. The sudden wealth sparked an investment boom in Britain and helped finance the Industrial Revolution—showing how deeply India’s economy shaped Britain’s rise.

Drainage system collected by British

Doctrine of Lapse
In the 19th century, the British introduced the Doctrine of Lapse, a policy designed to expand their territory even further. This was brought in by Lord Dalhousie, the Governor-General of India. According to Indian traditions, if a ruler had no natural male heir, he could adopt a son who would inherit the throne.

This practice was respected for centuries in Indian royal families. However, under the Doctrine of Lapse, the British refused to recognise adopted heirs. This meant that if a ruler died without a natural male son, his state would be automatically annexed by the East India Company. This policy ignored Indian customs and traditions, which deeply hurt the pride of rulers and their people.

[IMAGE_START] A black and white portrait sketch of a man with wavy hair, wearing a high-collared shirt and cravat. Caption: Lord Dalhousie [IMAGE_END]

Using this policy, the British annexed several important states, including Jhansi, Satara and Nagpur. Each annexation expanded British territory and reduced Indian kingdoms to nothing more than conquered land. The Doctrine of Lapse created widespread anger and distrust towards the British. Many people saw it as an unfair policy and an insult to Indian culture. This resentment later played an important role in sparking the 1857 Revolt, when Indians rose up against British rule.

Subsidiary Alliance
Another powerful strategy used by the British was the Subsidiary Alliance, introduced by Lord Wellesley around 1798. Under this system, Indian rulers had to agree to keep British troops stationed in their states ‘for protection’ and pay the heavy cost of maintaining them. Rulers also had to give up the right to have their own independent armies or make alliances with other powers.

[IMAGE_START] A colored portrait painting of a man seated in a chair, wearing a dark coat and white shirt/cravat. Caption: Lord Wellesley [IMAGE_END]

To make sure British control was secure, a Resident (a British official) was placed in the courts of Indian rulers. On paper, the rulers looked independent, but in reality, the Resident guided or controlled most of their decisions. This made the rulers completely dependent on the British and reduced their sovereignty to a mere show.

The ruler of Hyderabad was the first to accept this alliance in 1798. Soon after, other states like Awadh

1757 was a British general so he introduced

and Mysore were also forced to join. Once a state entered the Subsidiary Alliance, it could never leave without facing a powerful British military response. For the British, this was an easy way to control vast areas without directly ruling them—it created what was called an 'empire on the cheap.' The rulers had to bear the costs, while the British enjoyed the power.

This system was presented as a protective arrangement, but in truth, it made Indian rulers weak and powerless. Over time, the Subsidiary Alliance became one of the main reasons why the British were able to dominate most of India without facing united resistance.

LET'S EXPLORE

British Policies – Fair or Foul?
The British introduced policies like Doctrine of Lapse and Subsidiary Alliance.
* Write the name of each policy and its meaning in one line.
* Create two emoji-style symbols: one for fair and one for unfair, based on what you think.
* Write why you chose that symbol.

Purpose: This activity encourages critical thinking about British rule and its fairness.

[IMAGE_START] A cartoon illustration showing a British official standing on a high pedestal, holding a scroll or document. Below him, a crowd of Indian people are depicted, some bowing, some looking up. There are flags in the crowd. [IMAGE_END]

Company officials imposed very high cash taxes on farmers, demanding payment, regardless of whether harvests were good or bad. Farmers were forced to grow more cash crops like indigo and cotton instead of food grains, leaving little to eat during shortages. When poor harvests struck in 1770, the Bengal Famine followed, killing nearly 10 million people—almost one-third of the population. Shockingly, the Company did not reduce taxes and even raised them during the famine, showing complete disregard for Indian lives. This cruel behaviour was later criticised by some British officials and writers such as W.W. Hunter and William Digby, but by then, Bengal's society and economy had already been shattered.

The tragedy repeated itself many times in later years. One of the worst was the Great Famine of 1876–78, which killed up to 8 million people, mainly in the Deccan plateau and southern India. The famine was caused by drought, but its effects became deadly because of British policies. Traders hoarded grain to increase prices, while the colonial government followed its so-called 'free market' policy, refusing to interfere with food prices. In fact, during the famine, about one million tonnes of rice every year were exported to Britain, while people starved in India. The Viceroy, Lord Lytton, declared that prices should not be controlled and instead of focusing on relief, he organised a lavish Delhi Durbar in 1876, with a week-long feast for 68,000 officials, satraps and maharajas, while millions of Indians went hungry. Relief camps were opened in some areas, but they were far too few and gave only meagre rations. The Famine Commission of 1878–80 even argued that relief should be deliberately kept low, as giving more might make the poor 'dependent on government help.'

FROM PARADISE TO HELL? – ECONOMIC EXPLOITATION

The British conquest of India completely changed its economy, turning a land once described as a paradise into one of poverty and exploitation. Harsh taxation, unfair trade policies and the constant outflow of wealth weakened agriculture, destroyed industries and left millions of people in misery.

Following are some of the major ways in which colonial rule exploited India and reshaped its economy:

Famines
India suffered some of the worst famines in history under British rule and these disasters were made much worse by the policies of the colonial government. After its victory at Plassey in 1757, the East India Company gained the Diwani rights or the right to collect revenue, in Bengal, Bihar and Odisha. Robert Clive once called Bengal 'the paradise of the earth', but under Company rule, this paradise quickly became a land of suffering,

[IMAGE_START] An illustration of a beach scene with large sacks of grain stacked in the foreground. Carts and people are visible in the mid-ground. Buildings are in the background near the water. [IMAGE_END]

Grain bags on Madras beach, ready for export, while people were dying of hunger in large numbers.

Over the entire colonial period, India faced more than a dozen major famines and historians estimate

Marching option of 2000 by me...

Block market

that between 50 and 100 million people died in these tragedies—numbers comparable to the death toll of World War II. Famines had occurred in earlier times too, caused by droughts, floods or wars, but never on such a large and repeated scale. Under British rule, the constant pressure of high taxation, forced cash crop farming, grain exports and neglect of relief measures made famines far more deadly. Instead of protecting the people, colonial rule pushed rural India into deep poverty and long-term suffering, from which it struggled to recover even after independence.

[IMAGE_START] An illustration showing a large crowd of people, many appearing emaciated and weak, waiting outside a building with a gate. Some are sitting on the ground, others standing. A man is lying on a stretcher being carried. The scene depicts a famine relief camp. [IMAGE_END]
People waiting for famine relief in Bangalore (today Bengaluru) during the 1877 famine (Illustrated London News)

EXTENDED LEARNING
During the 1876–78 famine, the British government's strict 'free market' policy made food unaffordable for the poor. The same period saw Britain celebrating Queen Victoria's title as Empress of India. This cruel contrast between grandeur and starvation became a symbol of colonial hypocrisy.

Drain of Wealth
One of the biggest criticisms of British rule in India was the 'Drain of Wealth.' This idea explained how India's money, natural resources and profits from trade were regularly taken away to Britain without bringing any benefit to Indians. Leaders like Dadabhai Naoroji and Romesh Chunder Dutt studied this carefully. They showed that huge amounts of wealth were leaving India every year in the form of heavy taxes, profits from railways, payments for the British army and the high salaries and pensions of British officials. Naoroji, in his famous book Poverty and Un-British Rule in India (1901), used official British data to prove how India's riches were being drained. Similarly, Dutt's Economic History of India described how Indian industries and agriculture suffered under colonial policies. Even foreign scholars observed this injustice. In 1895, American historian Brooks Adams argued that Britain's Industrial Revolution—which made it the world's leading power—was partly financed by India's wealth. Another historian, Will Durant, even called this process the 'stolen wealth of India.' All of this showed how deeply India was being exploited.

[IMAGE_START] Portrait of Dadabhai Naoroji. Handwritten note: "Founder of Indian National Congress", "Grand Old Man of India". [IMAGE_END]
Dadabhai Naoroji

[IMAGE_START] Portrait of Romesh Chunder Dutt. [IMAGE_END]
Romesh Chunder Dutt

[IMAGE_START] Portrait of Brooks Adams. Handwritten note: "American Economist". [IMAGE_END]
Brooks Adams

[IMAGE_START] Portrait of Will Durant. [IMAGE_END]
Will Durant

Recent research gives us an even clearer picture of how much India lost. Economist Utsa Patnaik has estimated that between 1765 and 1938, India lost nearly 45 trillion U.S. dollars (in today's value) to Britain. To put this in perspective, this is about 13

times, the size of Britain's GDP in 2023. This vast sum was drained not just through taxation but also by making Indians pay for colonial expenses such as building the railways, telegraphs and postal systems, as well as for financing British wars in other countries. In simple terms, India was paying the cost of its own exploitation. If even a small part of this wealth had remained in India, it could have been invested in agriculture, industries, schools and hospitals, making India a far stronger country at the time of Independence. Instead, the continuous outflow of resources left India poor, underdeveloped and struggling with poverty, while Britain grew rich, industrialised and powerful.

Impact on Britain

While India was sinking deeper into poverty and suffering, Britain was becoming richer and stronger every year. The huge amount of wealth taken from India was not wasted—it was used to finance the Industrial Revolution in England, which changed Britain into one of the world's most powerful nations. Indian cotton, raw materials and tax revenues were sent to Britain and used to build factories, industries, railways and powerful weapons. Indian farmers, who once grew food for their own families, were forced to produce cotton and indigo for export, which then supplied British textile mills.

Cities like Manchester and Birmingham became rich industrial centres, producing large quantities of cloth, machinery and other goods, most of which were sold back to India and other colonies. In this way, Britain gained double profits—first by taking Indian raw materials cheaply and then by selling manufactured goods at high prices. The money drained from India also helped Britain to strengthen its navy and army, which gave it military superiority over many countries.

The contrast between the two nations was clear. Indian farmers and workers faced heavy taxation, famine and unemployment, while Britain enjoyed new prosperity, modern technology and improved living standards. This shows how colonialism turned India from a 'paradise of wealth and prosperity' into a land of misery and poverty, while at the same time turning Britain into one of the most advanced and industrialised countries of the 19th century.

CHANGING LANDSCAPES UNDER COLONIAL RULE

The arrival of the British brought massive changes in India's economy, governance, education and infrastructure. These changes were often presented as progress, but in reality, they mostly served the interests of the colonisers while weakening Indian society.

Following are some of the ways in which colonial rule reshaped India and left long-lasting effects:

Decline of Industries

Before the arrival of the British, India was recognised across the world as one of the most advanced manufacturing regions. Its industries included textiles, iron, steel, paper and handicrafts, all of which were admired for their skill and quality. Indian cotton textiles, in particular, were world famous for their fine weaving, bright colours and intricate designs. They were exported widely to Europe, Africa and Asia and ranged from ultra-thin muslins of Bengal to richly embroidered cloth from Gujarat. Along with textiles, Indian iron and steel products, pottery, jewellery and carpets were valued for their craftsmanship. These industries gave

Because of losing popularity? in the market

employment to millions of people and contributed to India's reputation as one of the richest lands of the pre-modern world.

Under British rule, however, these industries suffered a sharp decline. The British introduced policies that favoured their own machine-made goods and destroyed Indian competition. Heavy taxes and duties were imposed on Indian textiles entering Britain, while British cloth was imported into India with little or no tax. This created an unfair trade system that made Indian goods expensive and British goods cheap. At the same time, Britain controlled shipping routes and exchange rates, making it harder for Indian traders to sell abroad. By the 19th century, India's textile exports had collapsed, while imports of British factory-made products grew rapidly. Skilled communities of weavers and artisans, who had passed down their craft for generations, lost their jobs and were forced back into subsistence farming on heavily taxed land. The misery was so severe that Governor-General William Bentinck remarked in 1834: 'The bones of the cotton weavers are bleaching the plains of India.'

The decline was not limited to textiles alone. Traditional industries such as iron, steel and paper also collapsed because they could not compete with mass-produced goods from British factories. Over time, millions of skilled workers became unemployed and sank into poverty. Agriculture, which was already overburdened by high land taxes and cash crop farming, had to absorb these displaced workers, creating even more poverty in the villages. India's economy, once prosperous, steadily weakened. By the time of Independence in 1947, India's share of the world GDP had fallen to barely 5 per cent, compared to its leading position before the 18th century. In less than two centuries, British policies had transformed India from a global centre of wealth and industry into one of the poorest nations in the world, leaving behind long-lasting damage to its economy and society.

DID YOU KNOW?

By 1800, Indian textiles made up nearly 25% of the world's cloth trade, but by 1850, this had dropped to less than 2% due to British industrial policies. The city of Dhaka, once called the 'Manchester of the East,' turned into a ghost town within decades.

This made governance less personal and less responsive and it gave the British almost total control over administration.

The introduction of British laws and codes of justice further weakened India's traditional systems. For centuries, disputes were settled by panchayats and community elders, who based their decisions on customary practices and social norms that people understood and accepted. The British ignored these traditions and imposed a foreign legal system that was unfamiliar to the people. Although they claimed this was 'modernisation,' it actually created courts that were expensive, slow and conducted in languages like English or Persian, which most villagers could not understand. This meant that only the wealthy or those with access to lawyers could afford justice, while ordinary Indians often remained helpless. Over time, people grew alienated from the legal system and the centuries-old tradition of community-based self-rule and justice disappeared. Thus, colonial governance reduced people's participation in administration and ensured that the British enjoyed maximum authority, while Indians had little voice in how they were governed.

Education Transformation

Before the arrival of the British education reforms, India had diverse and well-established systems of learning that had existed for centuries. Local institutions such as pathshalas (Hindu village schools), madrasas (Islamic schools), viharas (Buddhist centres), gurukuls (teacher-led institutions) and apprenticeship systems provided education to children and young people.

[IMAGE_START] A painting depicting a traditional Indian Gurukul. A teacher (Guru) with a beard and orange robes sits under a large tree, reading from a book. Several students (Brahmacharis) sit on the ground in front of him, listening attentively. Some are holding sticks or staffs. The setting is outdoors, near a body of water with hills in the background. [IMAGE_END]

Gurukuls (teacher-led institutions)

These schools taught not only reading, writing and arithmetic but also practical skills, cultural values, religious teachings and traditional knowledge. British officials who surveyed India in the early 19th century were surprised to find hundreds of thousands of village schools still active. In Bengal and Bihar alone, between 100,000 and 150,000 schools were reported to be running up to 1830. These institutions followed simple, low-cost methods of teaching that were effective for local communities and ensured that education was not just for the elite but available to ordinary villagers as well.

A major turning point came in 1835, when Thomas Babington Macaulay introduced his famous Minute on Education. Although Macaulay admitted that he had 'no knowledge of Sanskrit or Arabic,' he strongly criticised Indian learning and declared European knowledge to be far superior. In his words, 'a single shelf of a good European library was worth the whole native literature of India and Arabia.' His goal was not to promote Indian traditions but to create a class of Indians who would serve as loyal supporters of British rule—people who would be 'Indian in blood and colour, but English in taste, in opinions, in morals and in intellect.' As a result, English replaced Indian languages as the medium of higher education and subjects like science, mathematics and law were introduced in place of Sanskrit, Persian or regional literature. Over time, traditional schools lost importance and English education became a mark of modernity and higher status in society.

[IMAGE_START] A black and white portrait of a man in formal 19th-century attire (dark coat, white shirt, cravat). He has receding hair and a serious expression. The caption below reads "Thomas Babington Macaulay". [IMAGE_END]

Thomas Babington Macaulay

This transformation in education served several colonial objectives. It created a 'group of English-educated Indians who could be employed as clerks, teachers, lawyers and minor officials in the colonial government,' saving the British a great deal of money as they did not have to hire many Europeans. At the same time, it weakened traditional systems of knowledge and learning, disconnecting Indians from their cultural roots and making them more dependent on colonial ideas. English education slowly came to be associated with power, prestige and job opportunities, while regional languages and local knowledge systems were ignored or undervalued. This created a growing cultural and social divide in India: on one side were the English-educated elites, who often secured jobs and gained influence and on the other side were the masses, who

had little or no access to this new form of education. Many historians argue that this policy allowed the British to control Indian minds as well as Indian resources, shaping how Indians thought, worked and even saw their own traditions.

DID YOU KNOW?

By 1857, India had three universities—at Calcutta, Bombay and Madras—founded on the model of the University of London. These institutions became the breeding ground for the first generation of Indian lawyers, reformers and freedom fighters who led the national movement.

[IMAGE_START] An illustration of a large, multi-story building with a prominent arched gateway. In front of the building, there are silhouettes of people and animals, including camels and horses, suggesting a bustling scene. [IMAGE_END]

Railways and Telegraphs

The British introduced railways and telegraphs in India during the 19th century. On the surface, these appeared to be signs of progress and modernisation, but their real purpose was to strengthen British economic and political control. The railway network was mainly designed to transport raw materials such as cotton, coal, jute, indigo and food grains from the interior of India to major ports like Bombay, Calcutta and Madras, from where they were shipped to Britain to feed its factories. At the same time, the railways made it easier to flood Indian markets with cheap, machine-made British goods, which ruined local industries and handicrafts. The telegraph system gave the British rulers the ability to communicate quickly across long distances, especially during times of war, revolts or famines, ensuring that no challenge to their authority could spread for long. In this way, both railways and telegraphs served as tools of control rather than instruments of people's welfare.

[IMAGE_START] A historical black and white photograph of a steam train engine. The engine is dark, with large wheels and a smokestack. It is positioned on tracks in front of a brick building. [IMAGE_END]

A steam locomotive of the Madras Railway (one of the companies operating the rail network in British India) in 1860

Although the railways did create some benefits—such as bringing distant regions together, connecting markets and allowing faster travel for ordinary people—their routes were not built to serve Indian needs. Traditional trade centres and routes were often ignored, while new railway lines were carefully planned to link resource-rich areas to ports for the benefit of British traders. A major military purpose was also clear: the British could now move troops, weapons and supplies quickly to defeat rebellions, such as the Revolt of 1857. This speed of movement gave them a major advantage in maintaining control over such a vast land. For Indians, however, the new system often meant heavier taxes, forced changes in agriculture and the loss of traditional livelihoods.

The construction of the railway and telegraph networks was also not a generous gift from the colonial rulers. Most of the expenses were taken directly from Indian tax revenues, meaning that Indians paid for projects that mainly served the British. Railway companies were guaranteed profits by the government, but when they ran into losses, it was the Indian taxpayers who covered the costs. Similarly, the telegraph network was maintained by Indian funds, yet its benefits were almost entirely for colonial administrators and the army. In fact, the entire colonial administration—its bureaucracy, military forces and the luxurious lifestyles of British officials—was financed through Indian money. Instead of investing in schools, hospitals or irrigation for Indian farmers, much of the revenue collected was diverted into maintaining this system of exploitation. In short, Indians were made to pay for their own subjugation, as the wealth of the country was used to strengthen British rule rather than improve Indian society.

EXTENDED LEARNING

The first railway line in India opened in 1853 between Bombay and Thane. Interestingly, the trains had separate carriages for Europeans and Indians, showing racial segregation even in infrastructure. The line's main purpose was to move cotton from interior regions to Bombay port for export.

THE GREAT REBELLION OF 1857

The Revolt of 1857, often called the First War of Independence or the Great Rebellion of 1857, was the most important uprising against British rule in the 19th century. The British referred to it as the 'Sepoy Mutiny' because it began with Indian soldiers, known as sepoys, who served in the East India Company's army under British officers. But historians today reject this term because the revolt was not just limited to the army. It soon grew into a large-scale movement involving peasants, artisans, dispossessed landlords and rulers, making it the first attempt at a united struggle against colonial rule. The rebellion revealed the deep frustration and anger that had been building for decades due to exploitation, heavy taxes and disrespect for Indian traditions.

The Great Rebellion of 1857

The causes of discontent went back many years. An early warning came in the Vellore Mutiny of 1806 in present-day Tamil Nadu, when sepoys revolted after being forced to follow new uniform rules that violated their religious customs, such as banning sacred marks and requiring them to shave their beards. Although the mutiny was defeated, it showed that Indian soldiers were unhappy with British interference in their personal and religious life. Most sepoys were from agricultural families and they saw how their relatives back home suffered under unfair land revenue policies, poverty and debt traps, which made them even more restless. The final spark came in 1857 when rumours spread that new rifle cartridges were greased with cow and pig fat, which insulted both Hindu and Muslim beliefs. At Barrackpore, a brave sepoy, Mangal Pandey, attacked his British officers in protest. Though he was executed, his actions inspired others. Soon after, at Meerut, sepoys killed their officers and marched to Delhi, where they declared the elderly Mughal emperor Bahadur Shah Zafar as their symbolic leader. The revolt spread rapidly to major centres like Kanpur, Lucknow, Jhansi and Gwalior, with courageous leaders such as Rani Lakshmibai, Nana Saheb, Tantia Tope and Begum Hazrat Mahal playing key roles in resisting British power.

The rebellion was marked by both heroic resistance and tragic violence. In Kanpur, rebels under Nana Saheb first promised safe passage to the British but later killed over 200 civilians, an incident still debated by historians. Across northern India, the rebels captured towns, fought fierce battles and tried to establish their control. But the British, with better weapons organisation and reinforcements, launched a systematic and brutal counterattack. Delhi was recaptured in September 1857 after days of bloodshed and the British carried out house-to-

house massacres. In the countryside, they burned villages, destroyed crops and executed thousands of people to spread fear. Despite their courage, the rebels lacked unified leadership, proper planning and modern equipment, which weakened their struggle. This disunity allowed the British to defeat them region by region.

Even though the revolt was eventually defeated, it became a turning point in Indian history. It was the first large-scale movement that showed Indians could rise together across caste, religion and region to challenge foreign domination. It planted the seed of Indian nationalism and inspired later leaders to continue the freedom struggle in new ways. In 1858, the British Crown took direct control of India from the East India Company, marking the beginning of the British Raj. The British reorganised the army to divide Indian soldiers by caste and region, making it harder for them to unite in the future. They also shifted their policies from aggressive territorial expansion to strengthening control over areas they already ruled. Although the Great Rebellion failed, it left behind a powerful legacy of unity, sacrifice and resistance, remembered as the first major step toward India's independence.

EXTENDED LEARNING

After the 1857 revolt, the British built several memorials like the Residency in Lucknow to glorify their 'victory'. In contrast, Indians remembered the sites of resistance—Jhansi, Kanpur and Delhi—as symbols of sacrifice. These places later became rallying points during the freedom movement.

[IMAGE_START] A drawing of a tree growing out of a rocky cliff or mountain face. The tree has green leaves and appears to be thriving despite the rocky environment. [IMAGE_END]

Two Brave Heroines

Rani Lakshmibai of Jhansi

Rani Lakshmibai of Jhansi is remembered as one of the most fearless leaders of the Revolt of 1857. When the British tried to annex her kingdom of Jhansi through the Doctrine of Lapse, she strongly opposed it and refused to surrender her state. She quickly gathered soldiers, trained women to fight and inspired common people with her leadership. With the support of Tatia Tope, the Maratha commander, she managed to escape from a surrounded Jhansi and later captured the Gwalior fort, including its

Preamble is adopted on 22nd Nov 1949

treasury and weapons. On 18 June 1858, she fought a fierce battle against the British and was killed while fighting bravely on the battlefield. Even her enemies admired her courage, calling her the 'best and bravest of the rebels.' Her determination, clever strategies and sacrifice made her a shining example of resistance against colonial rule. For generations, she has remained a symbol of courage and patriotism who inspired freedom fighters during India's independence movement.

[IMAGE_START] A black and white portrait of Rani Lakshmibai of Jhansi. She is depicted seated, holding a sword upright in her right hand. She is wearing traditional Indian attire, jewelry including a necklace and earrings, and a head covering. [IMAGE_END]
Rani Lakshmibai of Jhansi

Begum Hazrat Mahal of Awadh

Begum Hazrat Mahal was another brave and determined heroine of the 1857 revolt. After the British annexed Awadh and sent her husband, Nawab Wajid Ali Shah, into forced stay away from his kingdom, she took charge and became the leader of the struggle in Lucknow. She refused to accept British authority and organised soldiers, nobles and common people to defend her kingdom. With strong determination, she led the defence of Lucknow against British forces and fought with great courage. The British offered her safe passage if she gave up, but she boldly refused and continued to resist. When the Queen's proclamation of 1858 promised equality and religious freedom, Begum Hazrat Mahal warned Indians not to trust such promises, reminding them that the British had never kept their word. Even when she was forced to leave Lucknow and seek shelter in Nepal, she never gave up her spirit of resistance. Her leadership, wisdom and courage made her one of the most respected women leaders of the revolt, remembered for her sacrifice and patriotism.

[IMAGE_START] A black and white portrait of Begum Hazrat Mahal of Awadh. She is shown wearing a headscarf and large traditional jewelry, including earrings and a necklace. [IMAGE_END]
Begum Hazrat Mahal of Awadh

[IMAGE_START] A lightbulb icon with green leaves inside, connected by a wavy line to the text below. At the bottom left, there are small icons of a wind turbine, a car, and solar panels. [IMAGE_END]

KEY TERMS

Colonialism : The practice of one country taking control over another country's land and people. The stronger country rules, exploits resources and forces its own culture and system on the weaker one.

Imperialism : The policy of building a large empire by conquering or controlling other countries for power, wealth and influence. European nations like Britain and France followed this during the colonial age.

Monopoly : Complete control over trade or production by one person or company. Example: The Portuguese created a monopoly over the spice trade by forcing ships to buy passes called cartaz.

Navigation : The art and science of planning and controlling the route of ships. European sailors used advanced navigation tools to explore new sea routes and expand colonial trade.

Missionaries : People, often from Europe, who travelled to other countries to spread Christianity. They built churches and schools but also tried to change local beliefs and traditions.

Exploitation : The unfair use of people and resources for profit. Colonisers took India's raw materials like cotton and spices, earning wealth while leaving Indians poor.

Decolonisation : The process through which countries that were under colonial rule gained independence and self-rule. Example: India became independent from British rule in 1947.

Drain of Wealth : A term used by Indian leaders like Dadabhai Naoroji to describe how Britain took away India's money, raw materials and profits, making India poor and Britain rich.

A. Knowledge Based Questions

Integration of knowledge

I. Fill in the blanks.

  7. The Doctrine of ______, introduced by Lord Dalhousie, allowed the British to annex any Indian state whose ruler died without a natural male heir.

G. Competency Based Questions

I. Assertion - Reason Type Questions.

DIRECTIONS: In the following questions, a statement of Assertion (A) is followed by a statement of Reason (R). Mark the correct choice as:
a. Both Assertion (A) and Reason (R) are true and Reason (R) is the correct explanation of Assertion (A).
b. Both Assertion (A) and Reason (R) are true but Reason (R) is not the correct explanation of Assertion (A).
c. Assertion (A) is true but Reason (R) is false.
d. Assertion (A) is false but Reason (R) is true.

  1. Assertion (A): The Portuguese were the first Europeans to arrive in India by sea.
    Reason (R): Vasco da Gama reached Calicut in 1498, opening the sea route between Europe and Asia.

  2. Assertion (A): The Dutch were defeated by the forces of Travancore under Marthanda Varma in the Battle of Colachel.
    Reason (R): After this defeat, the Dutch expanded their rule to cover the whole of southern India.

  3. Assertion (A): The French and the British competed fiercely for political control in India during the 18th century.
    Reason (R): Both countries wanted to establish military superiority and monopolise trade in India.

  4. Assertion (A): The Great Rebellion of 1857 was completely successful in ending British rule in India.
    Reason (R): It united many soldiers and civilians against British exploitation and became a symbol of India's struggle for freedom.

II. Case Study Based Questions.

During his visit to a museum, Rohan learned about the Battle of Plassey (1757), which marked the beginning of British political control in India. The East India Company, led by Robert Clive, defeated Siraj-ud-Daulah with the secret support of Mir Jafar, gaining control over Bengal—the richest Indian province. This victory allowed the British to collect revenue, control trade, and slowly expand their rule. What began as trade soon turned into colonial domination, changing India's economy and governance for nearly two centuries.

Questions:
1. Why was the Battle of Plassey an important turning point in Indian history?
2. How did the East India Company benefit from its control over Bengal?
3. What role did Mir Jafar play in helping the British win the battle?
